Analysis of bulk-surface reaction-sorption-diffusion systems with Langmuir type adsorption
Björn Augner, Dieter Bothe

Abstract
. We consider a class of bulk-surface reaction-adsorption-diffusion systems, i.e. a coupled systems of reaction-diffusion systems on a bounded domain Ω ⊆ ℝ d \Omega\subseteq\R^{d} (bulk phase) and its boundary Σ = ∂ Ω \Sigma=\partial\Omega (surface phase), which are coupled via nonlinear normal flux boundary conditions. In particular, this class includes a heterogeneous catalysis model with Fickian bulk and surface diffusion and nonlinear adsorption of Langmuir type, i.e. transport from the bulk phase to the active surface, and desorption. For this model, we obtain well-posedness, positivity and global-in-time existence of solutions under some realistic structural conditions on the chemical reaction network and the sorption model.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35A01, 35D35, 35K57, 35Q92, 58J35

Version of August 10, 2026.

1.
Introduction

Over the last decades, bulk/surface-systems have attracted increasing attention in Life Science and Engineering, as an increasing number of effects, especially in Chemistry and Biology have been observed that involve and can be modelled and explained by volume/surface interaction: For example, subprocesses which take place localized on some surface or interface may influence processes within a fluid phase which is in contact with the surface or interface. More generally, interaction of processes on subdomains with distinct physical or environmental properties contribute to a wide range of phenomena: They give raise to polarization and pattern formation in cell biology, may increase the spreading speeds for animals, but also for immune biology, and in chemical engineering new catalytic materials with optimized structures facilitate an effective production of desired products and bulk/surface interaction is also the core phenomenon in wetting processes.
When mathematically modelling such processes, such systems can be abstractly described by a open set
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
and a d-1–dimensional manifold
\Sigma
, which is typically (part of) the boundary
\partial\Omega
(
surface
) of the domain
\Omega
or which divides disconnected regions of the open set
\Omega
(
interface
).
For instance, in cell biology, the formation of
polarities
, i.e. a break in symmetry, such as front/back-polarization as a preparatory step for cell migration, is believed to be governed by a
cascade of signalling components.
As a main contribution to cell migration, a localized peak of myosin concentration can be observed in the front of a cell. At the same time at the tail end the otherwise, i.e. for unpolarized cells, uniformly distributed concentration of actin is elevated.
The localisation of myosin at the front then triggers a local extension of the cell, the elevated actin concentration at the back a local contraction, so that effectively the cell moves in direction of its front, see e.g.
[
58
]
for an up-to-date review on modelling of single cell polarity, and the main targets of research connected to it.
As one of the from mathematical analysis viewpoint important works, let us exemplary describe the setting in M. Castanho and M. Fernandes
[
25
]
.
These consider a disc-shaped model domain for the
intracellular medium
, which is separated from the annulus-shaped
extracellular medium
by the semipermeable
cell membrane
.
This means that only some particular chemical substances may cross the cell membrane. Thus, some chemical components are bound to the intracellular or to the extracellular medium, respectively: For example, a
ligand
, i.e. a molecule with can form a receptor-ligand complex with a certain
receptor
, may be present in the extracellular medium, but is not able to cross the cell membrane. The receptor, on the other hand, may be produced at the cell membrane, but may only desorp into the intercellular medium. Ligand-receptor complexes, then, can only be formed when the ligand coming from the extracellular medium attaches to a receptor which is present at the cell membrane (Eley–Rideal mechanism), or by first adsorbing at the cell membrane, and subsequent activation, diffusion and formation of a ligand-receptor complex with a receptor (Langmuir–Hinshelwood mechanism).
The authors of
[
25
]
then proceed to show that a constant production rate of receptors at the cell membrane can lead to uniformly positive stationary solutions of the corresponding bulk/surface-system of reaction-diffusion equations.
For further read and related topics, we refer to references
[
10
]
,
[
28
]
,
[
32
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
51
]
and
[
70
]
.

Field-road models, on the other hand, have been proposed by Berestycki, Roquejoffre and Rossi
[
15
]
to model the propagation of wolves in certain regions of Canada, where the wolves employ men-made corridors (
road
) to invade new habitats (
field
). In this type of models, the field
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
corresponds to a region where the wolves usually live, and where they are assumed to diffuse, reproduce and die at certain rates, modelled by the reaction-diffusion equation

\partial_{t}u-d\Delta u=f(u),\quad t>0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega,

where
u(t,\boldsymbol{x})
decribes the population density at time
t\geq 0
and position
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega
.
In the context of field-road models, the reproduction process
f(u)
is typically modelled by a Kolmogorov–Petrovskii–Pisconov-model (
KPP-model
, for short), i.e. a function
f\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\R)
such that

f(0)=f(1)=0\quad\text{and}\quad 0<f(s)\leq sf^{\prime}(0)\quad\text{for }s\in(0,1),\quad f(s)<0\quad\text{for }s>1.

One particular important example is given by the choice
f(s)=s(1-s)
from Fisher’s equation which corresponds to logistic growth for the corresponding ODE.
The KPP-equation is well-studied, in particular it is known that, e.g. considering
\Omega=\R^{n}
, there is a finite
asymptotic speed of propagation

c_{\mathrm{KPP}}=2\sqrt{df^{\prime}(0)}
.
In the field-road model, this asymptotic speed of propagation may (or may not) be affected by additional diffusion on the road
\Gamma\subseteq\partial\Omega
, where members of the species may diffuse with a surface diffusion coefficient
d_{\Gamma}>0
, which is typically larger than
d
.
The exchange rates between the field and the road are then modelled as being linear w.r.t. the trace of the field population and the road population, respectively, viz. the normal flux condition

-d\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}|_{\Gamma}=\nu u|_{\Gamma}-\mu u_{\Gamma},\quad t>0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma.

In absence of natality or mortality on the road, the surface reaction-diffusion equation on the road reads as

\partial_{t}u_{\Gamma}-d_{\Gamma}\Delta_{\Gamma}u_{\Gamma}=\nu u|_{\Gamma}-\mu u_{\Gamma},\quad t>0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma

with
u_{\Gamma}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
denoting the population density on the road at time
t\geq 0
and position
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma
.
For the model case
\Omega=\R\times(0,\infty)
and
\Gamma=\partial\Omega=\R\times\{0\}
it has then been shown in
[
15
]
that for every bounded and non-negative initial datum
(u^{0},u_{\Gamma}^{0})
there exists a unique, bounded and non-negative solution
(u,u_{\Gamma})
to the field-road KPP-reaction-diffusion model, and, moreover, the diffusion on the field road may affect the asymptotic speed
c_{\ast}
of propagation in the following way:
For surface diffusion coefficients
d_{\Gamma}>0
below some certain threshold
d_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{crit}}>0
, the asymptotic speed of propagation for the field-road model will be the same as for the full-space KPP-reaction-diffusion equation, whereas for values
d_{\Gamma}>d_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{crit}}
above this threshold, the asymptotic speed of propagation is elevated, at least in directions
\pm\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
as these are the directions of the road.
In
[
16
]
, the same authors amended their model by a drift term and mortality on the road.
Tellini
[
71
]
, Rossi, Tellini and Valdinoci
[
60
]
and
[
27
]
considered other field domains and various choices for the roads as well as different choices for boundary conditions at the non-road parts of the field’s boundary.
Whereas the former two considered existence and parameter-dependency of the asymptotic speed of spreading, the latter focussed on existence of positive stationary solutions.
Berestycki, Ducasse and Rossi
[
14
]
consider the case, where an ecological niche on the road that is moving with some constant velocity
c_{\mathrm{niche}}\in\R
.

In chemical engineering, volume/surface-models are known and applied in practise for long.
Refining chemicals, polymerization as well as environmental protection
[
43
]
are only some of the tremendous applications of
catalysts
. To give a definition, we may e.g. follow Keil
[
43
]
who defines
catalysts
as ”materials that
accelerate
reactions and convert reactents
preferably
to desired products
without being consumed
themselves.”
This means that,
activity
, i.e. the ability to accelerate chemical reactions, and
selectivity
, i.e. the ability to produce close to none undesired by-products, are the most important key figures of a catalyst, and, moreover, the catalyst acts within
catalytic circles
, in which the catalysts stands at the start and the end of such a circle.
While
homogeneous
catalysts, especially those which are available at low cost, are still used in some industrial areas, the search for new, more effective, i.e. more active and more selective, catalysts nowadays is primarily focussed on
heterogeneous
catalysts, especially catalytic surfaces, usually consisting of a support material, on which a catalytic surface is prepared.
The support material helps to regulate heat and electron transfer, so that the catalysts remains in a regime where it operates most efficiently.
Often activity and selectivity of a solid catalyst are achieved by a porous structure which contains single atoms of nanoparticles of since
<20\mathrm{nm}

[
43
]
.
Models for such a pore structure range from straight pores, curved pores, randomly oriented pores in the Johnson–Steward model
[
41
]
up to Bethe-lattices, cf.
[
17
]
which have been introduced by Beeckmann and Froment
[
18
]
and Reyes and Jensen
[
59
]
into the context of heterogeneous catalysis.
These structural models can then be combined with numerical methods such as Monte-Carlo simulations to derive realistic free energy models for the adsorbed species, which can then be employed in continuum-thermodynamic models and combined with fluid flow models.
For more information on heterogeneous catalysis, we refer to the overview articles
[
56
]
by A. Renken and L. Kiwi-Minsker,
[
63
]
by R. Schlögel, and the handbook of heterogeneous catalysis
[
33
]
edited by G. Ertl, H. Knözinger and J. Weitkamp as well as to the books by T. Whirt
[
72
]
and M.G. White
[
73
]
. For an overview on recent advances in computational methods for heterogeneous catalysis, see B.W.J. Chen, L. Xu and M. Mavrikakis
[
26
]
.

Another highly relevant example of bulk-surface systems/processes is the field of dynamic wetting. There, a liquid is in contact with a solid support and the so-called contact line, i.e. the triple-line at which liquid, solid and the surrounding gas phase meet, moves along the solid wall. While standard engineering descriptions try to model the moving contact line via a (semi-)empirical relation between the contact angle, i.e. the angle between the liquid-gas and the liquid-solid interface, and the speed of normal displacement of the contact line due to its motion along the solid, more sophisticated models include the exchange of mass between the bulk liquid and the liquid-solid interface as well as the liquid-gas surface. See
[
65
]
and the monograph
[
66
]
for this so-called Interface Formation Model. For more information see
[
34
]
and the literature which is cited in these references.
All of the models also include the two-phase Navier-Stokes equations. This makes the overall model rather complex and mathematically demanding. While the analysis of such models is out of the scope of the present paper, a more fundamental mathematical analysis of bulk-surface reaction-sorption-diffusion systems can help to understand their properties.

In the present manuscript, we consider bulk-surface reaction-sorption-diffusion models and mathematically analyse the well-posedness (in the sense of existence and uniqueness of solutions and continuous dependence on the initial datum) for a large class of such bulk-surface systems.
In particular, we provide a global-in-time existence result under some structural conditions on the system, viz. a
triangular structure
(also known as
intermediate sum condition

[
50
]
) of the chemical reaction network and
polynomial boundedness
conditions on the chemical reaction network in the bulk phase and on the active surface.
In view of mass conservation, it is natural to consider a normal flux condition for the coupling between the bulk phase and the active surface.
The class of allowed non-linear relations will include the special case of a nonlinear
Langmuir
type model for adsorption and desorption at the active surface.
We include the local-in-time well-posedness analysis for more general classes of sorption models, which may, e.g., correspond to more general models for the surface chemical potentials.

2.
A brief literature overview

We next give a brief overview on some relevant papers on the mathematical theory of reaction-diffusion-systems with an emphasis on results related to local-in-time-existence, to regularity and to global-in-time existence of solutions and works which are related to the present one.
The different branches in the theory of reaction-diffusion-systems are, therefore, so plenty that we do not even try to give a complete overview over all of them, but rather focus on the development of the theory of reaction-diffusion-systems from a mathematical analyst’s point of view with a particular bias on regularity and well-posedness results (in the sense of initial-boundary value problems) both of local and global-in-time type.
That means, we focus on the analysis of reaction-diffusion system.
Due to their historical importance for the development of the research field, we will start with some of the milestones in the theory for pure bulk diffusion.
Though, most of the time, no bulk/surface interaction has been considered in these works, the techniques developed there, nevertheless, provide useful orientation for the bulk/surface theory.
Here, the fundamental works of Ladyshenskaja, Solonnikov and Ural’ceva
[
46
]
remain up to today a tremendous corner-stone, which provide optimal regularity results in Hölder space and Sobolev space settings to linear, but possibly inhomogeneous parabolic equations and systems.
Further results in this perspective can be found in N.D. Alikakos’ article
[
1
]
.
H. Amann extensively studied quasilinear parabolic equations, with an emphasis on reaction-diffusion systems in
[
5
]
and on global-in-time existence of solutions in
[
4
]
.
In the context of quasilinear equations, let us also mention the seminal paper
[
54
]
by J. Prüss.
R. Denk, M. Hieber and J. Prüss in their works
[
29
]
,
[
30
]
treated the linear vector-valued case.
Based on such
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity results and boot-strapping methods, the Boundedness-Implies-Continuation property has been employed in works such as
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
by H. Amann,
[
38
]
by D. Henry,
[
61
]
by F. Rothe,
[
40
]
,
[
49
]
,
[
52
]
by M. Pierre and co-authors as well as
[
24
]
,
[
57
]
and
[
69
]
, typically in combination with
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-estimates derived by duality methods, to obtain global-in-time existence results.
Note that for the more realistic case of a Maxwell–Stefan diffusion model for multi-component fluid mixtures, the mathematical analysis is more involved due to its quasi-linear nature and the summation constraint on the diffusive mass fluxes, see the contribution
[
20
]
of the second author of this paper.

For bulk/surface systems, some of the abstract optimal regularity results for linear parabolic systems have been generalised to the vector-valued case with bulk-surface-coupling in
[
31
]
by R. Denk, J. Prüss and R. Zacher.
For the case of a bulk reaction-diffusion system with nonlinear boundary conditions resulting from a fast surface chemistry limit, see
[
11
]
and
[
12
]
by the paper’s authors.

In recent years, bulk-surface reaction-diffusion systems have drawn increasing attention of several research groups, see the aforemention active research areas in life science and engineering.
Obviously this is just a short glimpse on an abundant reservoir of literature on this topic from that perspective.
The mathematical analysts’ perspective on bulk/surface systems seems to be comparably underrepresented, given the tremendous importance of the subject in view of developments in, e.g., modern bio-technologies.
Let us mention some recently published articles, where the authors mainly consider the global-in-time well-posedness of bulk-surface systems (sometimes also called volume-surface systems).
O. Souček et al.
[
68
]
considered bulk/surface systems within a thermodynamically consistent framework, with particular emphasis on diffusion and sorption phenomena.
For example, the second author, M. Köhne, S. Maier and J. Saal
[
23
]
consider a bulk-surface reaction diffusion system with Fickian type diffusion in a cylindrical bulk phase and on parts of its surface, amended with surface chemical reactions.
Additionally, they imposed inflow and outflow boundary conditions on the molar fluxes
\boldsymbol{j}_{i}
corresponding to the molar concentrations
c_{i}
on a
transmissive part

\partial\Omega\setminus\Sigma
and no flux boundary conditions on the boundary
\partial\Sigma
(in the sense of manifolds) between the active and transmissive part of the boundary.
In
[
51
]
, B. Niethammer, M. Röger and J.J.L. Velázquez¸ start from a bulk-surface reaction-diffusion-model with a Henry type sorption law and a Michaelis–Menten type surface reaction model plus an external stimulus signal.
A main part of their research is not to stop at the question of global-in-time well-posedness of that particular system under consideration, but to derive polarisation models for suitable scaling of the parameter.
In fact, they can show that under certain conditions these models have
critical masses
for polarisation.
Similar models have also been considered, e.g. in
[
36
]
and
[
13
]
.
On the frontier of global-in-time existence, and for a much more general class of bulk-surface reaction diffusion systems, quite recently J. Morgan and B.Q. Tang
[
50
]
addressed reaction-diffusion systems where the bulk and the surface dynamics are coupled via a non-linear Robin-type boundary condition.
Their main ingredients to obtain global-in-time existence of solutions are an assumption on
mass control
and an
intermediate sum condition
, similar to those conditions which we impose in this work.
Let us in detail comment on similarities and differences in comparing their results to ours.
Regarding the general structure of the system under consideration, we also consider a bulk-surface system of reaction-diffusion equations which are coupled by some kind of sorption mechanism. Whereas in our case, we include a (given) macroscopic flux
\boldsymbol{u}
in our system, leading to an advective term
\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{c}_{i}
in the balance equation for each component
c_{i}
, Morgan and Tang consider the basic case where the macroscopic flux vanishes, i.e.
\boldsymbol{u}=0
.
An inspection of their technique of proof, however, indicates that they could have also included such a convective term in their analysis, essentially without changing the flow of their arguments.
Also the general conditions on the sorption and reaction rates, which can be paraphrased as local Lipschitz continuity and quasi-positivity, correspond to each other in both works.
The most essential differences can be observed concerning generality of the functions modelling chemical reaction rates and sorption rates, and on the regularity of the initial data.
On the one hand, Morgan and Tang demand less structure on the sorption rates, without any assumption on a diagonal structure of the sorption rates, but they do demand that the initial data for the system lie in some
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}
-Sobolev–Slobodetskii space, and – in contrast to us – they demand that
p>d
which immediately implies some Hölder-regularity on the initial data, and, hence, the solution.
In our case, on the other hand, we can also handle initial data in the class
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}
for some
p<d
.
Therefore, already establishing local-in-time existence of strong solutions in the class
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
requires considerable effort in our setting, whereas in
[
50
]
the initial data are only assumed to be attained in the sense that solutions lie in the class
\mathrm{C}([0,\tau);\mathrm{L}_{p})
.
As a by-product, we also have to impose more regularity in the formulation of the polynomial growth condition, and our polynomial growth conditions are, at first, growth conditions on the derivatives of the functions modelling chemical reaction and sorption rates, and only by the fundamental theorem of calculus these imply growth conditions on the respective functions itself, as they have been directly imposed in
[
50
]
.
In this sense,
[
50
]
and the present article focus on different, but closely related aspects of bulk-surface reaction-diffusion-sorption systems, and whereas the setting in
[
50
]
is more general from a structural point of view, we can handle a more notion of solutions, which allows for´ less regular initial data.
Note that in
[
50
]
, the regularity of initial data might possibly be relaxed to initial data of class
\mathrm{L}_{\infty}
, but even this class will in general not include all functions of class
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}
if
p<d
is allowed for.
This different setting is also reflected in the different proof technique for the global-in-time existence result.
Similarly to the strategy in
[
50
]
, but also to works focusing on pure bulk reaction-diffusion systems such as
[
52
]
, the key strategy for establishing global-in-time existence of solutions is the derivation of
\mathrm{L}_{q}
–growth bounds, and, first for finite
q\in(1,\infty)
and finally for the case
q=\infty
.
As it is quite typical for such problems, we will use
duality methods
, but – in contrast to the main trend – we will also include dual norms in the duality method based estimates that will be applied, cf. especially Lemma
8.5
and Lemma
8.6
.

The manuscript is organised as follows:
In Section
1
, we fix the general framework and comment on some conditions on the structure and smoothness which we impose on the models for the chemical reactor, its active surface and the physical-chemical processes such as advection, diffusion, chemical reactions in the bulk and on the surface and adsorption and desorption at the boundary.
Thereafter, we will give an overlook over the linear theory of
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity for parabolic equations, on which the application to semi-linear problems is based on.
Then, in Sections
6
and
7
, we demonstrate that the solution to positive initial data is (just as one would expect) positive and only thereafter we show local-in-time well-posedness, i.e. existence and uniqueness of solutions (at least on some small time interval) and continuous dependence of the solution on the initial datum.
To prepare a boot-strap argument, we derive several auxiliary results, including dual estimates and
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates in Section
8
, which will become crucial when actually proving global-in-time existence of solutions in Section
9
.

3.
Modelling assumptions

Before introducing the concrete class of models under consideration, let us indicate how to derive this model, starting from general continuum-thermodynamic balance equations of reaction-diffusion-systems.
Thereby, we will also introduce some notation and physical interpretation of the variables appearing in the equations.
We consider reaction-diffusion systems both in the bulk phase, i.e. inside the domain
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
, and on its active surface, given by (a part of) its boundary
\Sigma\subseteq\partial\Omega
.
For example, one may think of a chemical reactor, consisting of the interior bulk phase and a catalytic surface on the boundary.
The general balance equations for the individual concentrations
c_{i}
of a substance
A_{i}
in a fluid mixture within the bulk phase read as

\partial_{t}c_{i}+\dv(c_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{i})=r_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})\quad\text{in }(0,\infty)\times\Omega,

where,
c_{i}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
denotes the (molar) concentration of species
A_{i}
at position
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega
and time
t\in\R
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
its individual continuum velocity.
Moreover, we write
\boldsymbol{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})^{\mathsf{T}}
.
The individual velocities
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
can, in the most general form of a model, obtained from individual momentum balance equations, .
In the more case of a single momentum balance, also considered here, the diffusive part
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}=\boldsymbol{v}_{i}-\boldsymbol{v}
will be modelled based on thermodynamic principles, where
\boldsymbol{v}
the barycentric velocity of the fluid mixture.
In the latter case, the individual velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}=\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
of each fluid mixture component can be decomposed into an advective part
\boldsymbol{v}
, and a (frame-indifferent) diffusive part
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
.
When it comes to thermodynamic consistent models, it will be important that the diffusive part will be modelled observation frame indifferent, cf.
[
19
]
, whereas the advective part
\boldsymbol{v}
will be depend on the observation frame.
Denoting by
\boldsymbol{j}_{i}^{\mathrm{diff}}:=c_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
the (molar-based)
diffusive mass flux
, the general balance equations for reactive-fluid mixtures take the form

\partial_{t}c_{i}+\dv(c_{i}\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{j}_{i}^{\mathrm{diff}})=r_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})\quad\text{on }(0,\infty)\times\Omega,

where
r_{i}
models chemical reaction rates.
We will employ Fick’s model for the diffusive flux, i.e. we let

\boldsymbol{j}_{i}^{\mathrm{diff}}=-d_{i}\nabla c_{i}

with some species-dependent (constant) diffusivity
d_{i}>0
.
At least for dilute systems, i.e. systems where the concentration of the solvent is by far higher than the concentrations of the solutes which are dissolved in the solvent, this gives quite a good approximation to reality.

For modelling the advective part, i.e. the barycentric velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
, there are various approaches, such as the Navier–Stokes equations, the Stokes equations or Darcy’s balance equation.
Here, however, we will assume the macroscopic vector field
\boldsymbol{v}
to be given.
Let us note that under appropriate conditions on
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
the advective part can be treated as a perturbation in the analysis to come.

The reaction-diffusion model within the bulk phase reads

\partial_{t}c_{i}+\dv(c_{i}\boldsymbol{v}-d_{i}\nabla c_{i})=r_{i}(\boldsymbol{c}),\quad i=1,\ldots,N,

which in the
incompressible
case, i.e.
\dv v=0
, and for constant diffusivities
d_{i}>0
can also be written as

\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-d_{i}\Delta c_{i}=r_{i}(\boldsymbol{c}),\quad i=1,\ldots,N.

Note that we do
not
include the solvent in the model.
Let us remark that for higher concentrations of the solutes more delicate models also include cross-diffusion effects, such as the Fick–Onsager or the Maxwell–Stefan model, are available.
The mathematical analysis of such cross-diffusion systems will be the topic of further research in the area of bulk-surface reaction-diffusion-sorption systems, as it is more involved due to its quasilinear nature.
Also note that results as in
[
39
]
indicate that for low concentrations, the Maxwell–Stefan diffusion model is very well approximated by the linear Fickian diffusion model.

Similarly, on the surface, the general balance equation for reaction-diffusion-systems reads

\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\Sigma}+\dv_{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{j}_{i}^{\Sigma})=r_{i}^{\Sigma}+s_{i}^{\Sigma},

where the additional term
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
corresponds to
sorption processes
, i.e. the exchange of mass between the bulk phase and the boundary.
Here, we will assume that the boundary of the chemical reactor is
impermeable
, i.e. no particles may cross
\Sigma
, so that, identifying
A_{i}^{\Sigma}
with an adsorbed version of the species
A_{i}
, we obtain the constraint

s_{i}^{\Sigma}=c_{i}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=c_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})+\boldsymbol{j}_{i}^{\Sigma,\mathrm{diff}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu},

i.e.
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
is the normal part of the individual mass flux near the boundary.
We will further restrict our attention to the case where the given macroscopic velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
is subject to no-flux boundary conditions, i.e. we assume that

\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0\quad\text{on }\Sigma,

and, consequently, the constraint on the sorption rate reduces to

s_{i}^{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{j}_{i}^{\Sigma,\mathrm{diff}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}.

On the one hand, this may serve as a
definition
of the sorption rate
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
, but, on the other hand, we may use
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
to impose an adsorption-desorption
model
and, thereby, to close the system; this will be done below.
For the surface flux, we consider the special case of vanishing surface velocity
\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{0}
(the model will not include the solid material constituting the catalytic surface) and model the surface diffusive fluxes by
\boldsymbol{j}_{i}^{\Sigma}=-d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
for the surface gradient
\nabla_{\Sigma}
, i.e. we employ a Fickian type diffusion model also on the surface.

To sum up, we consider bulk-surface reaction-sorption-diffusion models of the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-\dv(d_{i}\nabla c_{i})

\displaystyle=r_{i}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\infty)\times\Omega,\,i=1,\ldots,N,

(1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=r_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})+s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\infty)\times\Sigma,\,i=1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle-d_{i}\partial_{\nu}c_{i}

\displaystyle=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\infty)\times\Sigma,\,i=1,\ldots,N,

where
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
is a (sufficiently regular) bounded domain, serving as a model for a (prototypical) chemical reactor.
Moreover,
\Sigma\subseteq\partial\Omega
denotes a (sufficiently regular) part of the boundary of
\Omega
, and which later on will be interpreted as an
active part
of the reactor surface.
In this paper, for simplicity, we will consider the special case that the active surface constitutes the whole boundary of the chemical reactor, i.e.
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
.

Remark 3.1
.

In the research paper
[
23
]
the authors restrict themselves to the special, but in practical applications relevant case of
cylindrical
domains
\Omega
and (in chemical engineering most relevant) spatial dimension
d=3
, whereas
\Omega
may be any bounded
\mathrm{C}^{2}
-domain in
\R^{d}
here.
The additional geometrical constraint helps in
[
23
]
to include inflow and outflow conditions at the bottom resp. top of the cylindrical domain in the local well-posedness analysis, i.e. for
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity theory.
As for such domains a fully developed
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity theory is available, the observations made in the present paper, however, will carry over to the case of inflow and outflow boundary conditions for a cylindrical domain, too.

To get a complete and consistent model, we write the sorption rates
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
between bulk and surface as
s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
, where
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
is a function which in general may depend on two
\R^{N}
-valued variables.
These functions reflect the model we impose for the adsorption and desorption mechanisms.

Below, we will state the most relevant conditions on the ‘externally” given ‘velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
, the diffusion coefficients
d_{i}
,
d_{i}^{\Sigma}
and the reaction and sorption models
r_{i}^{\Sigma}
and
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
.
For the moment, however, let us start with some realistic modelling assumptions on the sorption model and, in particular, compare these with the special case of a Langmuir adsorption model which constitutes a highly relevant model for ad- and desorption at an active surface:

(1)

As a function of
c_{i}
and
c_{i}^{\Sigma}
,
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
is continuously differentiable twice, and its first and second derivatives are uniformly bounded on
\R^{2}
.
As we shall see, we have to weaken this condition to include Langmuir type adsorption.

(2)

For fixed surface concentration, we expect that the higher the bulk concentration, the higher the adsorption rate of that respective component.
That is, we demand that the function
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
increases in the argument
c_{i}
.

(3)

For fixed bulk concentration, the desorption rate increases by increasing the surface concentration of the respective component.
Therefore, we assume that the functions
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
decrease in arguments
c_{k}^{\Sigma}
.

(4)

Moreover, we expect adsorption and desorption not to increase faster than linearly, if we increase the bulk resp. the surface concentration.
Hence, we assume that the function
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
is bounded linearly both from below and from above, in the sense that there are constants
k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}},k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}>0
such that

-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}z_{i}^{\Sigma}\leq s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma})\leq k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}z_{i}\quad\text{for}\quad\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma}\in\R_{+}^{N}.

Typically, we will write the effective sorption rate as the difference between the adsorption rate and the desorption rate, i.e.

s_{i}^{\Sigma}=s_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}-s_{i}^{\mathrm{de}},

where we expect
s_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}
and
s_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}
to be functions which are non-negative on
(0,\infty)^{2}
, and conditions
2
and
3
will typically mean that
s_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma})
is bounded from above by
k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}z_{i}
, whereas
s_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma})
is bounded from above by
k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}z_{i}^{\Sigma}
.
This, in particular, means that the adsorption rate is at most linearly growing w.r.t. the boundary trace of the bulk concentration
c_{i}|_{\Sigma}
and, on the other side, the desorption rate is at most growing linearly w.r.t. the surface concentration.
All these assumptions are obviously met by the so-called Henry model for ad- and desorption, where ad- and desorption simply depend linearly on the trace of the bulk concentration and the surface concentration, resp., i.e.

s_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(c_{i}|_{\Sigma},c_{i}^{\Sigma})=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}|_{\Sigma},\quad s_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}(c_{i}|_{\Sigma},c_{i}^{\Sigma})=k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}c_{i}^{\Sigma}.

The disadvantage of this model is that it does not take into account that (in the relevant case of relatively high surface concentrations and a finite capacity on the surface) for adsorption of a molecule to the surface, enough empty space on the surface is necessary.
In this sense, it is only a realistic model for low concentrations on the active surface, whereas for higher surface concentrations it will be more realistic to assign a maximal capacity
c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty}>0
to the surface, and to consider the well-known Langmuir model
[
47
]

s_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(c_{i}|_{\Sigma},c_{i}^{\Sigma})=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\left(1-\theta(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\right),\quad c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\geq 0,\,0\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma}\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty},

where
\theta(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})=\frac{1}{c_{S}^{\Sigma,\infty}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
denotes the occupancy number on the surface w.r.t. to some final number
c_{S}^{\Sigma}
of available sites per area element.
This first ansatz, however, has a serious drawback: The mass-action-kinetics models for the reaction rates
r_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
and the Fickian diffusive flux model
-d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
do, in general, not prevent the weighted sum of surface concentrations
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
to go above the threshold
c_{S}^{\Sigma,\infty}
, as for systems, in general,
no maximum principle
is valid.
To overcome this problem, there are at least two options available: On the one hand, we might refine the reaction-rate model and the diffusion model to prevent the case
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}c_{i}(t,y)>c_{S}^{\Sigma,\infty}
.
This can be achieved, for example, by modelling the chemical reaction rates based on the Langmuir model for the free energy and diffusive mass fluxes by a Maxwell–Stefan or Fick–Onsager model.
Alternatively, we may extend the domain of
r_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}
to
\R^{2}
by setting

s_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma})=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}z_{i}\left(1-\theta(\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma})\right)^{+},\quad\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma}\in\R_{+}^{N},

where
s^{+}:=\max\{0,s\}
is the positive part of a scalar
s\in\R
.
Let us compare the properties of this particular sorption model with the ad-hoc assumptions from above:
First of all, the function
s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
depends on (the boundary trace of) the bulk concentration
c_{i}
and the surface concentration
c_{i}^{\Sigma}
of the
i
-th species and it is increasing in the first and decreasing in the second component.
The upper and lower linear bounds
-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}c_{i}^{\Sigma}\leq s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})\leq k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}
hold true for all non-negative concentrations
c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma}\geq 0
as well. However, concerning regularity, in the Langmuir sorption model, the function
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
is neither a
\mathrm{C}^{2}
-function, nor are its first and second derivative (which exist almost everywhere) uniformly bounded on
\R^{2}
.
We therefore aim to prove local-in-time existence and, thereafter, global existence results under less restrictive growth and regularity conditions.
This will allow us to handle the Langmuir adsorption without, say, replacing the Langmuir adsorption by more regular functions as it has been done, e.g., in
[
23
]
.
Furthermore, we allow for chemical reactions in the bulk phase, constituting an additional source or sink term
r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})
on the right-hand side of the reaction-diffusion-advection equations in the bulk phase, viz.

\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-\dv(d_{i}\nabla c_{i})=r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})\quad\text{in }(0,\infty)\times\Omega,\,i=1,\ldots,N.

Similar to reaction-diffusion-systems in the bulk phase with, say, no-flux boundary conditions, the semilinear term can be handled using maximal regularity results to establish local-in-time existence of solutions.
However, it may very well influence the long-time behaviour of the reaction-diffusion-system, so that we have to impose additional structural conditions to prevent a blow-up of the solution in finite time.

4.
On the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-theory for the linearised problem

The reaction-sorption-diffusion systems under consideration are of semilinear nature: The diffusive fluxes in the bulk phase (
\boldsymbol{j}_{i}=-d_{i}\nabla c_{i}
) and on the surface (
\boldsymbol{j}_{i}^{\Sigma}=-d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
) depend linearly on the (gradient of the) bulk resp. surface concentrations
c_{i}
resp.
c_{i}^{\Sigma}
.
The effective sorption rate
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
at the boundary constitute a, in general nonlinear, coupling between the two dynamics.
The models for the chemical reaction rates
r_{i}^{\Omega}
and
r_{i}^{\Sigma}
in the bulk phase and on the surface, respectively, depend (nonlinearly) on the bulk resp. surface concentrations.
Therefore, as for bulk reaction-diffusion-advection systems with, say, no-flux boundary conditions, we first revisit the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity theory of a linearised version of the system.
In fact, by the diagonal nature of the diffusion matrices
\boldsymbol{D}=\operatorname{diag}(d_{i})\in\R^{N\times N}
and
\boldsymbol{D}^{\Sigma}=\operatorname{diag}(d_{i}^{\Sigma})\in\R^{N\times N}
, this problem can easily be reduced to the question of
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity for the respective scalar equations, for which a complete theory is available.

We use the following notation: For
p\in(1,\infty)
, we denote

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{1}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{2}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{p}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})\qquad

(max. reg. space in the bulk phase)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{1}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{2}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})\qquad

(max. reg. space on the surface)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T)\times\Omega)=\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})\qquad

(base space in the bulk phase)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma;\R^{N})=\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})\qquad

(base space on the surface)

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2p}}}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})\qquad

(Neumann trace space)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2p}}}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{2-\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})\qquad

(Dirichlet trace space)

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N}),\mathrm{W}^{2}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N})\big)_{1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}},p}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{2-\nicefrac{{2}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N}),\qquad

(phase space in the bulk phase)

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}),\mathrm{W}^{2}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N})\big)_{1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}},p}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{2-\nicefrac{{2}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N})\qquad

(phase space on the surface)

with the short hand notations
\Omega_{T}:=(0,T)\times\Omega
and
\Sigma_{T}:=(0,T)\times\Sigma
and
\cong
denotes equality up to isometric isomorphisms; here, by identifying
f(t)(\boldsymbol{x})
with
f(t,\boldsymbol{x})
.
We use standard notation, i.e. we denote by
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)
,
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{k}(\Omega)
,
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{s}(\Omega)
Lebesgue, Sobolev and Sobolev–Slobodetskii spaces, resp., and by
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;E)
,
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{k}(\Omega;E)
and
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{s}(\Omega;E)
(
E
any real or complex Banach space) their respective vector-valued versions.
Also
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)
,
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{k}(\Sigma)
,
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{s}(\Sigma)
denote the corresponding spaces w.r.t. the surface measure
\sigma
on the boundary
\Sigma
, which is a (sufficiently regular) submanifold of dimension
d-1
without boundary (in the sense of manifolds).
We refer, e.g., to
[
37
]
for an overview on (the more general case of) Sobolev spaces over Riemannian manifolds and density, embedding and compactness theorems.

Note that in the linearised version of the bulk-surface reaction-diffusion-advection systems, the problems in the bulk phase (inhomogeneous Neumann problem) and on the surface are decoupled.
The Neumann problem is classical and can be handled even in the vector-valued case; see
[
30
, Theorem 2.1]
for a very general result on parabolic initial-boundary value problems in domains and
[
55
, Theorem 6.4.3]
for a version for compact hypersurfaces without boundary (also see
[
8
, Theorem 1.23]
for a much more general result on uniformly regular Riemannian manifolds).
The following
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity result can, therefore, easily be derived from the well-known scalar theory.

Lemma 4.1

(
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity)
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
be a bounded
\mathrm{C}^{2}
-domain,
p\in(1,\infty)
,
T_{0}>0
,
T\in(0,T_{0}]
,
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}_{s}((0,T_{0});\mathrm{W}_{r}^{1}(\Omega))
with
r,s\in(1,\infty)
such that
1-\frac{2}{s}-\frac{d}{r}>0
and
d_{i}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
,
d_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\Sigma)
be such that
d_{i}>0
and
d_{i}^{\Sigma}>0
.
The quasi-autonomous, linear problem

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-\dv(d_{i}\nabla c_{i})

\displaystyle=f_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,T)\times\Omega,\,i=1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(d_{i}^{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=f_{i}^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma,\,i=1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle-d_{i}\nabla c_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}

\displaystyle=g_{i}^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma,\,i=1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle(c_{i}(0,\cdot),c_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot))

\displaystyle=(c^{0}_{i},c^{\Sigma,0}_{i})

has a unique solution
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
if and only if
(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{f}^{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{g}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\F_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)\times\mathbb{G}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
,
(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Omega}\times\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Sigma}
, and, additionally in case
p>3
,
-d_{i}\nabla c_{0,i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=g_{i}^{\Sigma}|_{t=0}
(in the trace sense) on
\Sigma
for
i=1,\ldots,N
.
Moreover, there is a constant
C_{p,T_{0}}>0
, which is independent of the data
(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{f}^{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{g}^{\Sigma})
,
\boldsymbol{v}^{0}
and
\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma,0}
and of
T\in(0,T_{0}]
, such that

\big\|(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)}\leq C_{p,T_{0}}\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)}+\big\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)}+\big\|\boldsymbol{g}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathbb{G}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)}+T^{-1/p}\big\|(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\big\|_{\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Omega}\times\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Sigma}}\big).

In particular, the operators
A_{i}:\dom(A_{i})\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)
and
A_{i}^{\Sigma}:\dom(A_{i}^{\Sigma})\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)
defined by

\displaystyle A_{i}

\displaystyle=-\dv(d_{i}\nabla\cdot),\qquad

\displaystyle\dom(A_{i})=\{c_{i}\in\mathrm{W}^{2}_{p}(\Omega):\,-d_{i}\nabla c_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0\,\text{ on }\partial\Omega\}\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega),

\displaystyle A_{i}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=-\dv_{\Sigma}(d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}\cdot),\qquad

\displaystyle\dom(A_{i}^{\Sigma})=\mathrm{W}^{2}_{p}(\Sigma)\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)

generate bounded analytic and strongly continuous semigroups on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)
and
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)
.

As a consequence of Lemma
4.1
, the block-diagonal operators

\displaystyle A=\operatorname{diag}(A_{i})_{i=1}^{N}:

\displaystyle\dom(A)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\dom(A_{i})\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N}),

\displaystyle A^{\Sigma}=\operatorname{diag}(A_{i}^{\Sigma})_{i=1}^{N}:

\displaystyle\dom(A_{i}^{\Sigma})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\dom(A_{i}^{\Sigma})\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N})

generate bounded analytic
\mathrm{C}_{0}
-semigroups on their respective underlying Banach spaces, for every
p\in(1,\infty)
.

5.
Assumptions on the Semilinear Problem

We list assumptions to be imposed on the reaction and sorption models and draw first conclusions, e.g. concerning properties of the associated Nemytskii-operators on
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-spaces.

Assumption 5.1

(Regularity and Growth Bounds)
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
be fixed.
We group the assumptions in those on the (macroscopic) velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
, on the surface reaction rates
r_{i}^{\Sigma}
, on the bulk reaction rates
r^{\Omega}_{i}
and on the sorption model
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
.

(1)

Assumptions on the velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
of the fluid mixture (regularity, incompressibility, no-flux boundary condition):

(
A^{\mathrm{advec}}
)

\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{U}_{\tilde{p}}^{\Omega}(T):=\big\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{\tilde{p}}(\Omega_{T};\R^{d}):\,\dv\boldsymbol{u}=0\text{ in }\Omega_{T},\,\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0\text{ on }\Sigma_{T}\big\},

for some
\tilde{p}\in(d+2,\infty)
.
Then, in particular,
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathrm{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega};\R^{d}))
.

(2)

Assumptions on the chemical surface reaction rates
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
(regularity, quasi-positivity and polynomial bounds):

(
A_{F}^{\mathrm{ch}}
)

\displaystyle r_{i}^{\Sigma}=r_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\quad\text{for some function}\quad r_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\operatorname{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty)^{N};\R)\quad\text{for each }i=1,\ldots,N.

(
A_{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
)

\displaystyle r_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{z})\geq 0\text{ for each }\boldsymbol{z}\in[0,\infty)^{N}\text{ with }z_{i}=0,\quad i=1,\ldots,N.

There are constants
M^{\Sigma}>0
and
\gamma^{\Sigma}\geq 1
with
\begin{cases}\gamma^{\Sigma}\geq 1\text{ arbitrary},&\text{if }p\geq\tfrac{d+1}{2},\\
\gamma^{\Sigma}\leq(1-\tfrac{2p}{d+1})^{-1},&\text{if }p\in(1,\tfrac{d+1}{2})\end{cases}
s.t.

(
A_{P}^{\mathrm{ch}}
)

\displaystyle\big|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\big|\leq M^{\Sigma}(1+\big|\boldsymbol{y}\big|^{\gamma^{\Sigma}-1})\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{y}\in[0,\infty)^{N}.

(3)

Assumptions on the chemical bulk reaction rates
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}
(regularity, quasi-positivity and polynomial growth bounds):

(
{A_{F}^{\mathrm{ch}}}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle r^{\Omega}_{i}=r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})\quad\text{for some function}\quad r^{\Omega}_{i}\in\operatorname{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty)^{N};\R)\text{ for each }i=1,\ldots,N.

(
{A_{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})\geq 0\quad\text{for each }\boldsymbol{z}\in[0,\infty)^{N}\text{ with }z_{i}=0,\quad i=1,\ldots,N.

Moreover, there are constants
M^{\Omega}>0
and
\gamma^{\Omega}\geq 1
with

\begin{cases}\gamma^{\Omega}\geq 1\text{ arbitrary},&\text{if }p\geq\frac{d+2}{2},\\
\gamma^{\Omega}\leq(1-\tfrac{2p}{d+2})^{-1},&\text{if }p\in(1,\tfrac{d+2}{2})\end{cases}

such that

(
{A_{P}^{\mathrm{ch}}}^{\prime}
)

\big|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega})^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{y})\big|\leq M^{\Omega}(1+\big|\boldsymbol{y}\big|^{\gamma^{\Omega}-1})\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{y}\in[0,\infty)^{N}.

(4)

Assumptions on the
ad- and desorption
rates
\boldsymbol{r}^{\mathrm{sorp}}
(regularity, monotonicity and partial linear upper and lower bounds):
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
it holds that

(
{A_{F}^{\mathrm{sorp}}}
)

\displaystyle s_{i}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\quad\text{for some function }s_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\operatorname{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\R_{+}^{2N};\R)\quad\text{ such that}

(
A_{P}^{\mathrm{sorp}}
)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\big|s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\check{a}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\check{b}}_{i})\big|\leq C\big(1+\big|\boldsymbol{\check{a}}_{i}\big|^{K^{\Omega}}\big|\boldsymbol{\check{b}}_{i}\big|^{K^{\Sigma}}\big),\\
\big|\partial_{a_{i}}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\big|\leq C\big(1+\big|\boldsymbol{a}\big|^{K^{\Omega}-1}\big|\boldsymbol{b}\big|^{K^{\Sigma}}\big)&\text{and}\\
\big|\partial_{b_{i}}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\big|\leq C\big(1+\big|\boldsymbol{a}\big|^{K^{\Omega}}\big|\boldsymbol{b}\big|^{K^{\Sigma}-1}\big)&\text{for all }\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\R_{+}^{N},\end{cases}

for some
K^{\Omega},K^{\Sigma}\in\N
such that

\displaystyle d+1

\displaystyle\geq K^{\Omega}(d+1-p)^{+}+K^{\Sigma}(d-p)^{+}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{\max\big\{\frac{d+1}{2},\frac{(K^{\Omega}-1)^{+}}{(2K^{\Omega}-1)^{+}}(d+2)\big\},\frac{(K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}-1)(d+1)-1}{2(K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma})-1}\right\},

where we write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\check{v}}_{i}

\displaystyle:=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1},0,v_{i+1},\ldots,v_{N})\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{v}\in\R^{N}\text{ and }i=1,\ldots,N.

(
A_{M}^{\mathrm{sorp}}
)

\displaystyle s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})

\displaystyle\text{ increases in the variable }a_{i}\text{ and decreases in the variable }b_{i}.

There are constants
k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}},k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}>0
such that

\displaystyle-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}(1+\big|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big|)

\displaystyle\leq s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\leq k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1+\big|\boldsymbol{c}\big|)

(
A_{B}^{\mathrm{sorp}}
)

\displaystyle s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\leq 0,\quad s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i}^{\Sigma})\geq 0\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\in\R_{+}^{N}.

For the particular case of a Langmuir sorption model, we may then identify integrability parameters
p\in(1,\infty)
which fulfill the conditions stated in Assumption
5.1
on the sorption terms.

Example 5.2
.

The standard multi-component Langmuir sorption model

s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}):=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}(1-\theta)^{+}-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}c_{i}^{\Sigma},\quad\text{where }\theta:=\frac{1}{c_{S}^{\Sigma}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}c_{j}^{\Sigma}

for some
\sigma_{j}>0
,
j=1,\ldots,N
, and
c_{S}^{\Sigma}>0
fits into the class considered in assumption
5.1
by choosing functions
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})}{\partial c_{i}}

\displaystyle=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1-\theta)^{+}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})}{\partial c_{i}^{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle=-k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}\chi_{[\theta\leq 1]}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{c_{S}^{\Sigma}}-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}\leq 0\quad\text{for all }(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\R_{+}^{2N}.

From the first term (where
K^{\Omega}=K^{\Sigma}=1
), we obtain that the conditions on the sorption rate model in Assumption
5.1
are met, provided

p\geq\max\{\frac{d}{2},\frac{d}{3}\}=\frac{d}{2}.

Example 5.3

(Further Sorption models)
.

Several sorption rates resulting from different models for the surface chemical potential, cf.
[
45
, Table 7.4]
, fall in the class considered in the present paper, i.e. satisfy assumption
5.1
:

(1)

Henry model:
s_{i}^{\Sigma}=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
;

(2)

Volmer model:
s_{i}^{\Sigma}=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}\big(1-\theta\big)^{+}\exp\big(-\beta\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\big)-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
for some
\beta>0
;

(3)

Frumkin model:
s_{i}^{\Sigma}=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}\theta^{\sigma_{i}}\exp\big(-\sigma_{i}\beta\theta\big)-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
for some
\sigma_{i}\geq 1
and
\beta>0
;

(4)

Van-der-Waals model:
s_{i}^{\Sigma}=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}\exp\big(-\beta\theta\big)\exp\big(-\sigma_{i}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\big)-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
for some
\beta,\sigma_{i}>0
,

where, whenever
\theta
appears, it is defined as
\theta:=\frac{1}{c_{S}^{\Sigma}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}c_{j}^{\Sigma}
.
In all of these models we may choose
K^{\Omega}=K^{\Sigma}=1
, so that from Assumption
5.1
we get the constraint
p\geq\frac{d}{2}
.
Also note that all of these models have the general form
s_{i}^{\Sigma}=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma},\theta)
, so that for checking the monotonicity condition it suffices to check that

\frac{\partial s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma},\theta)}{\partial c_{i}}\geq 0,\quad\frac{\partial s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma},\theta)}{\partial c_{i}^{\Sigma}}\leq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma},\theta)}{\partial\theta}\leq 0

since
\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial c_{i}^{\Sigma}}\geq 0
.

Remark 5.4
.

Note that, by the (weak form of the) fundamental theorem of calculus, the polynomial growth conditions on the derivatives of
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}
,
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
and
\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}
in (

A
P

ch

), (

A
P

ch

′

) and (

A
P

sorp

) directly imply polynomial growth bounds on the functions themselves.
For example, for the surface chemical reaction rates, we obtain

\displaystyle\big|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{y})\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{0})+\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(s\boldsymbol{y})\,\mathrm{d}s\big|\leq\big|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(0)\big|+\big|\boldsymbol{y}\big|\int_{0}^{1}\big|\nabla\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(s\boldsymbol{y})\big|\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\big|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{0})\big|+\big|\boldsymbol{y}\big|\int_{0}^{1}M^{\Sigma}(1+s\big|\boldsymbol{y}\big|)^{\gamma^{\Sigma}-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\big|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{0})\big|+\frac{M^{\Sigma}}{\gamma^{\Sigma}}(1+\big|\boldsymbol{y}\big|)^{\gamma^{\Sigma}}

for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in[0,\infty)^{N}
, and, similarly, for the sorption rates

\displaystyle\big|s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\big|

\displaystyle=\big|s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i}^{\Sigma})+\int_{0}^{c_{i}}\partial_{a_{i}}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i}+a_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{i},\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i}^{\Sigma})\,\,\mathrm{d}a_{i}+\int_{0}^{c_{i}^{\Sigma}}\partial_{b}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i}^{\Sigma}+b_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{i})\,\,\mathrm{d}b_{i}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\big(1+\big|\boldsymbol{c}\big|^{K^{\Omega}}\big|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big|^{K^{\Sigma}}\big).

Absolutely continuous functions with polynomial growth bounds on their derivatives can always be written as a product of Lipschitz continuous functions, as the following auxiliary result shows.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\psi\in\mathrm{W}^{1}_{1,\mathrm{loc}}(\R^{m};\R)
be polynomially bounded with polynomially bounded derivatives, i.e. there are
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}\geq 0
such that

(2)

\big|\psi(\boldsymbol{s})\big|,\big|\partial_{i}\psi(\boldsymbol{s})\big|\leq C\prod_{j=1}^{m}(1+s_{j}^{2})^{\alpha_{j}/2}\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{s}\in\R^{m},\,i=1,\ldots,m

for some
C>0
.
Then
\psi
has the representation

(3)

\psi(\boldsymbol{s})=\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{s})\prod_{j=1}^{m}(1+s_{j}^{2})^{\alpha_{j}/2}\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{s}\in\R^{m},

where
\psi_{0}
is bounded and globally Lipschitz-continuous.

Remark 5.6
.

Note that, on the other hand, every function of the form (
3
) is polynomially bounded in the sense of equation (
2
).

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

We have to verify that

\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{s}):=\psi(\boldsymbol{s})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m}(1+s_{j}^{2})^{-\alpha_{j}/2},\quad\boldsymbol{s}\in\R^{m},

is a Lipschitz-continuous function.
Since
\psi
is weakly differentiable and each of the functions
(1+s_{j}^{2})^{-\alpha_{j}/2}
is classically continuously differentiable,
\psi_{0}
is weakly differentiable, so that it remains to check that its derivatives are uniformly bounded.
In fact,

\big|\partial_{i}\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{s})\big|=\big|\partial_{i}\psi(\boldsymbol{s})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m}(1+s_{j}^{2})^{\alpha_{j}/2}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\alpha_{i}s_{i}}{(1+s_{i}^{2})^{1/2}}\psi(\boldsymbol{s})\prod_{j=1}^{m}(1+s_{j}^{2})^{\alpha_{j}/2}\big|\leq C(m+1)<\infty

for all
\boldsymbol{s}\in\R^{m}
.
∎

Similarly, we may conclude the following structural property of the sorption rates
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
.

Corollary 5.7
.

Each of the sorption terms
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, may be written as a sum of functions
f
of the form

(
A_{S}^{\mathrm{sorp}}
)

f(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma})=\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma})\prod_{k=1}^{K^{\Omega}}\psi_{k}(u_{i})\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{K^{\Sigma}}\psi^{\Sigma}_{k}(u_{i}^{\Sigma}),

where each of the functions
\psi_{k}
,
\psi_{k}^{\Sigma}:\R\rightarrow\R
is Lipschitz continuous and
\psi_{0}:\R^{2}\rightarrow\R
is bounded and Lipschitz continuous.

Thanks to the polynomial growth bounds on the weak derivatives of
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}
and
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
, these functions act as continuous Nemytskii-operators between suitable
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-spaces.

Remark 5.8

(Reaction terms acting as Nemytskii Operators)
.

By the polynomial growth bounds (

A
P

ch

) and (

A
P

ch

′

) imposed on the bulk and surface chemistry reaction rate models, the Nemytskii operators corresponding to the functions
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
and
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}
and their derivatives induce nonlinear maps as follows:
For any
p\in[1,\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}:\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N}),

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}:\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}/(\gamma^{\Sigma}-1)}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N\times N}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}:\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N}),

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega})^{\prime}:\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}/(\gamma^{\Omega}-1)}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N\times N}).

Moreover, for each
\delta,T>0
, there are constants
k^{\Sigma}(\delta,T),k^{\Omega}(\delta,T)>0
such that

\displaystyle\big\|({\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{T})}

\displaystyle\leq k^{\Sigma}(\delta,T)

for all
\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}/(\gamma^{\Sigma}-1)}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})
with
\big\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}/(\gamma^{\Sigma}-1)}(\Sigma_{T})}\leq\delta
,

\displaystyle\big\|({\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}})^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{T})}

\displaystyle\leq k^{\Omega}(\delta,T)

for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}/(\gamma^{\Omega}-1)}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})
with
\big\|\boldsymbol{u}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}/(\gamma^{\Omega}-1)}(\Omega_{T})}\leq\delta
.

Hence,

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma}\in B_{\delta}(0)}\big\|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}/(\gamma^{\Sigma}-1)}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N\times N})}\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq k^{\Sigma}(\delta,T)\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})},\quad\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{z}^{\Sigma}\in B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N}),

and

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{z})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in B_{\delta}(0)}\big\|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega})^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}/(\gamma^{\Omega}-1)}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N\times N})}\big\|\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{z}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq k^{\Omega}(\delta,T)\big\|\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{z}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})},\quad\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{z}\in B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N}),

i.e. the Nemytskii operators associated to
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
and
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}
are Lipschitz continuous on each bounded subset of these spaces, respectively.

Proof.

Let us consider the model for the surface reaction rates
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
. Properties of the model for the bulk reaction rates
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}
can be derived analogously.
Observe that for every
\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})
, by using the polynomial boundedness of
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
, we may estimate

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{T})}^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\big|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))\leq M^{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\big(1+\big|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big|^{\gamma^{\Sigma}}\big)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}M^{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\big(1+\big|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big|^{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))=2^{p}M^{\Sigma}\big(T\sigma(\Sigma)+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T})}^{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}\big),

where
\sigma(\Sigma)
denotes the (finite) surface measure of
\Sigma
.
This shows that the Nemytskii operator associated to
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
maps
\mathrm{L}^{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})
into
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\big\|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\nicefrac{{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}}{{\gamma^{\Sigma}-1}}}}^{\nicefrac{{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}}{{\gamma^{\Sigma}-1}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\big|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\big|^{\nicefrac{{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}}{{\gamma^{\Sigma}-1}}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))\leq M^{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\big(1+\big|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big|\big)^{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}M^{\Sigma}\big(T\sigma(\Sigma)+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}}^{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}\big),

so that
(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}
has the claimed mapping property.
From here, the remaining estimates follow easily by the fundamental theorem of calculus, Tonelli’s theorem and Hölder’s inequality:

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})}^{p}=\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\big|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\big|\int_{0}^{1}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}(s\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\cdot(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\,\mathrm{d}s\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\big|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}(s\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\cdot(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\big|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}(s\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\cdot(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\big\|\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})}^{p}\big\|(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma})^{\prime}(s\boldsymbol{c}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\nicefrac{{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}}{{\gamma^{\Sigma}-1}}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N\times N})}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\big(k^{\Sigma}(\delta,T)\big\|\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})}\big)^{p}.

∎

6.
Positivity of solutions

In this section, we are concerned with an aspect which is quite important from the modelling perspective:
We show that strong solutions to the reaction-diffusion-advection-sorption system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-\dv(d_{i}\nabla c_{i})

\displaystyle=r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,T)\times\Omega,

(RDASS)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})+r_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle-d_{i}\nabla c_{i}\cdot\nu

\displaystyle=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle(c_{i}(0,\cdot),c_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot))

\displaystyle=(c^{0}_{i},c^{\Sigma,0}_{i})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for }i=1,\ldots,N

will be positive, provided the initial data are positive.
Let us start with fixing the solution concept used in this manuscript.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
.
A pair
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
of functions
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})
and
\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})
is called
strong
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
–solution
of (
RDASS
), if
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})|_{t=0}=(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})
in the trace sense and the differential equations in (
RDASS
) are satisfied for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\Omega
and
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\Sigma
, respectively.

Remark 6.2
.

Note that in related papers different solution concepts have been used, e.g.
classical solutions
in
[
50
]
, where the authors demand that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{\infty}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))

\displaystyle\quad\cap\big(\mathrm{C}^{(1,2)}((0,T)\times\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{C}^{(1,2)}((0,T)\times\Sigma;\R^{N})\big).

Away from zero, they thus demand more regularity (classical instead of weak derivatives), whereas close the initial time
t=0
they only demand continuity w.r.t. values in
\mathrm{L}_{p}
, but the strong solutions will at least be continuous w.r.t. values in the trace space
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}
.

We have not yet shown that such strong solutions exist, but we nevertheless can give a first result on positivity of solutions.

Lemma 6.3

(Positive solutions)
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
T>0
be given.
Let assumption
5.1
hold true.
If all components of the initial data
(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Omega,+}\times\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Sigma,+}
are (a.e.) non-negative and
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
form a strong
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
-solution of the reaction-diffusion-advection-sorption system (
RDASS
), then
c_{i}\geq 0
and
c_{i}^{\Sigma}\geq 0
a.e. on
(0,T)\times\Omega
and
(0,T)\times\Sigma
, resp.

Proof.

A similar result can be found in
[
23
, Lemma 5.2]
, where, however, a more restrictive regularity condition than (

A
F

sorp

) is demanded of the sorption model.
Nevertheless, the proof of
[
23
, Lemma 5.2]
essentially carries over, except for the following steps, where we have to employ the adjusted condition (

A
F

sorp

).

The structural and regularity assumptions on
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
can be used to prove that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Sigma}(\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(c_{i}^{\Sigma})-\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(c_{i}))s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\leq 0,

where
\phi_{\varepsilon}(r):=-r\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon/r}\chi_{[r\leq 0]}
is an
\varepsilon
-regularised (
\varepsilon>0
) version of the function
-r\chi_{[r\leq 0]}
and has the derivative
\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(r)=(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{r}-1)\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon/r}\chi_{[r<0]}
.
In particular, its derivative
\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
is monotone and
\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(r)\rightarrow-\chi_{[r<0]}
converges pointwise as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+
.
For any fixed time
t\in(0,T)
and representative functions
\boldsymbol{c}
and
\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}
, we decompose the surface
\Sigma
into the three pairwise disjoint sets
[\sign c_{i}(t,\cdot)=\sign c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)]
,
[c_{i}(t,\cdot)<0\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)]
and
[c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)<0\leq c_{i}(t,\cdot)]
, where we set
\sign(s)=1
if
s\geq 0
and
\sign(s)=-1
otherwise.
Recall that, by assumption
5.1
,
s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})
is increasing in the variable
a_{i}
and decreasing in the variable
b_{i}
, respectively.
We also recall the notation
\boldsymbol{\check{v}}_{i}:=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1},0,v_{i+1},\ldots,v_{N})
for vectors
\boldsymbol{v}\in\R^{N}
and
i=1,\ldots,N
.
Employing Lebesgue’s theorem on dominated convergences (and using that
\big\|\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\big\|_{\infty}=\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}\big|\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s)\big|=1
for all
\varepsilon>0
), we obtain by letting
\varepsilon>0
tend to zero that

\displaystyle\int_{[\sign c_{i}(t,\cdot)=\sign c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)]}(\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{c}_{i}^{\Sigma})-\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(c_{i}))s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}0,

\displaystyle\int_{[c_{i}(t,\cdot)<0\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)]}(\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))-\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(c_{i}(t,\boldsymbol{x})))s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\quad\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{[c_{i}(t,\cdot)<0\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)]}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{[c_{i}(t,\cdot)<0\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)]}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})\leq 0,

\displaystyle\int_{[c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)<0\leq c_{i}(t,\cdot)]}(\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))-\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(c_{i}(t,\boldsymbol{x})))s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\quad\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}-\int_{[c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)<0\leq c_{i}(t,\cdot)]}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\quad\leq-\int_{[c_{i}(t,\cdot)<0\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)]}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{\check{c}}_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})\leq 0.

Therefore, the proof of
[
23
, Lemma 5.2]
almost literally extends to the situation considered here, provided we neglect bulk chemistry, i.e.
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})=\boldsymbol{0}
.
In the case
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}\not=\boldsymbol{0}
, the same techniques as have been used for the
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
-term can be employed to show that for the additional terms we have the estimate

\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(c_{i})r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq 0.

Therefore, strong solutions to positive initial data are positive.
∎

7.
Local-in-time well-posedness

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
, initial data
(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Omega}\times\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Sigma}
and a finite time horizon
T>0
be given.
We now consider local-in-time well-posedness for the initial-boundary value problem (
RDASS
).
To do so, we formally rewrite (
RDASS
) as an abstract semilinear evolution equation of the form

(4)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}_{T,i}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})&=\mathcal{N}_{T,i}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\quad\text{subject to the constraint}\\
(c_{i}(0,\cdot),c_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot))&=(c^{0}_{i},c^{\Sigma,0}_{i})\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,N,\end{cases}

where the maps
\mathcal{L}_{T,i}
(linear part) and
\mathcal{N}_{T,i}
(nonlinear part) are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}:\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)\times\mathbb{G}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T),

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-\dv(d_{i}\nabla c_{i}),\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma}),-d_{i}\nabla c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})_{i=1,\ldots,N},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{T}:\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)\times\mathbb{G}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T),

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c}),\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})+\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}),\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})).

Observe that
\mathcal{L}_{T}
combines the action of the linear diffusion-advection operators in the bulk phase and on the surface, as well as the Neumann trace map, corresponding to the non-reactive case, whereas
\mathcal{N}_{T}
contains the non-linear contributions from bulk and surface chemical reactions and the sorption near the boundary.

As a first step, we reduce this semilinear problem with inhomogeneous initial data to a zero time-trace problem, i.e. to the special case where
(c^{0}_{i}|_{\Sigma},c^{\Sigma,0}_{i})=0
and
s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c^{0}_{i}|_{\Sigma},c^{\Sigma,0}_{i})=0
.
To this end, we solve the quasi-autonomous problems

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}_{T,i}(c_{i}^{\ast},c_{i}^{\Sigma,\ast})&=(0,0,s_{i}^{\ast})\\
(c_{i}(0,\cdot),c_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot))&=(c^{0}_{i},c^{\Sigma,0}_{i}),\end{cases}\quad i=1,\ldots,N,

where the function
s_{i}^{\ast}
on the right-hand side,

(5)

s_{i}^{\ast}(t,\cdot)=\mathcal{R}_{\R^{d}\rightarrow\Sigma}\mathrm{e}^{tA_{i}^{\Sigma}}\mathcal{E}_{\Sigma\rightarrow\R^{d}}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0}),\quad t>0,

is defined via the analytic semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{tA_{i}^{\Sigma}})_{t\geq 0}
generated by the scalar differential operator
A_{i}^{\Sigma}
on the surface, cf. Lemma
4.1
, and where
\mathcal{E}_{\Sigma\rightarrow\R^{d}}\in\B(\mathrm{W}^{1-\nicefrac{{3}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma);\mathrm{W}^{1-\nicefrac{{2}}{{p}}}_{p}(\R^{d}))
is some fixed continuous extension operator from
\Sigma
to
\R^{d}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\R^{d}\rightarrow\Sigma}
is the surface trace operator which can be considered as an operator in the class
\B(\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot\frac{1}{2}}_{p}(\R^{d}_{T});\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}_{p}(\Sigma_{T}))
