of bounded linear operators between the spaces
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot\frac{1}{2}}_{p}(\R^{d}_{T})
and
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}_{p}(\Sigma_{T}))
, cf.
[
62
]
.
For each component
i=1,\ldots,N
, the elliptic surface differential operator in divergence form,
A_{i}^{\Sigma}=\dv_{\Sigma}(d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}\cdot)
, defined on the domain
\dom(A_{i}^{\Sigma})=\mathrm{W}^{2}_{p}(\Sigma)
generates an analytic semigroup on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)
.
Moreover, by
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity of the corresponding parabolic initial-boundary value problem, it follows that
\boldsymbol{s}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{G}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)=\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})
if and only if

s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathrm{W}^{1-\nicefrac{{3}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma)\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,N.

Indeed, if
s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1-3/p}(\Sigma)
, then
(I-A_{i}^{\Sigma})^{-(2p+1)/2p}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Sigma)
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\big[\big((t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{tA_{i}^{\Sigma}}(I-A_{i}^{\Sigma})^{-(2p+1)/2p}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})

\displaystyle=(I-A_{i}^{\Sigma})^{-(2p+1)/2p}\mathrm{e}^{tA_{i}^{\Sigma}}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\big)\big]\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T})

for every
T>0
, and in this case
\big(t\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{tA_{i}^{\Sigma}}s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\big)\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
.

In our case, we are given initial data
(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathrm{W}^{2-\nicefrac{{3}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{W}^{2-\nicefrac{{2}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N})
and the general sorption model is of class
s_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}^{1}_{\infty,\mathrm{loc}}(\R^{2N};\R^{N})
.
Unfortunately this may, in general, not be enough to ensure that
s_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathrm{W}^{1-\nicefrac{{3}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma)
.

Remark 7.1
.

In general, the continuous embedding
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma)
(by which we mean that the trace operator is a continuous linear operator between the first and the latter space) is only true for
q\in[1,q^{\ast})
below some critical value
q^{\ast}<\infty
, and, thus, the function
(u|_{\Sigma})^{k}
will in general not be in the class
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)
(choose, e.g.,
k\in\N
such that
\frac{1}{p}<\frac{k}{q*}
).
Therefore, without further structural conditions we cannot infer from
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{W}^{2-2/p}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N})
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Sigma;\R^{N})
and
S\in\mathrm{W}^{1}_{\infty,\mathrm{loc}}(\R^{2N})
that
S(\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)
, or, even,
S(\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{W}^{1-3/p}_{p}(\Sigma)
.

Also note that in the critical case
p\leq\frac{d+2}{2}
the space
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}
will not embed into
\mathrm{L}_{\infty}
, in general, in contrast to the – in this regard – simpler situation, e.g. in
[
50
]
, where always
p>d
, so that in particular
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)
continuously embeds into some Hölder space
\mathrm{C}^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
for certain
\alpha>0
.

However, as the general assumptions imply that
s_{i}^{\Sigma}
is of some
generalised polynomial structure
, such a property can be ensured by using the following (special case of a more general) result of Amann
[
6
, Theorems 2.1 & 4.1]
, and which has been extended by Köhne and Saal, cf. e.g.
[
44
, Theorem 1.2]
, to functions mapping into a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
, cf.
[
42
]
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let a natural number
m\in\N
, scalars
s_{1},\ldots,s_{m},s\in(0,\infty)
,
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in(1,\infty)
and Banach spaces
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m},X
of class
\mathcal{HT}
be given.
Assume that there exists a continuous multiplication

\cdot:X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{m}\rightarrow X.

This induces the pointwise defined continuous multiplication operator, acting as

\mathrm{C}_{0}(\R\times\R^{d};X_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{C}_{0}(\R\times\R^{d};X_{m})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\R\times\R^{d};X).

We denote by

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}_{j}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{ind}(\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{j}}_{p_{j}}(\R\times\R^{d};X_{j}))=2s_{j}-\frac{d+2}{p_{j}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{ind}(\mathrm{W}^{s}_{p})(\R\times\R^{d};X)=2s-\frac{d+2}{p}

the
anisotropic Sobolev indices
of the Sobolev–Slobodetskii spaces
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{j}}_{p_{j}}(\R\times\R^{d};X)
and
\mathrm{W}^{s}_{p}(\R\times\R^{d};X)
, respectively.
There is a unique continuous extension of the multiplication operator introduced above to a multiplication operator

\cdot:\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{1}}_{p_{1}}(\R\times\R^{d};X_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{m}}_{p_{m}}(\R\times\R^{d};X_{m})\rightarrow\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s}_{p}(\R\times\R^{d};X),

if the following conditions are satisfied:

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\leq\min_{j=1,\ldots,m}s_{j}\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{regularity constraint})

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p_{j}}\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{integrability constraint})

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\min_{j=1,\ldots,m}\operatorname{ind}_{j},&\text{if all }\operatorname{ind}_{j}\geq 0\\
\sum_{j:\operatorname{ind}_{j}<0}\operatorname{ind}_{j},&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{index constraint})

with a strict inequality in the index constraint if at least one of the Sobolev indices
\operatorname{ind}_{j}
equals zero.

By means of localisation, flattening of the boundary and interpolation, we obtain the following result for sufficiently smooth
(d-1)
-dimensional manifolds, which, in particular, includes the case of boundaries
\partial\Omega
of regular bounded domains
\Omega
.

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
T>0
,
d\in\N
,
d^{\mathcal{M}}\leq d
, and
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\R^{d}
be a compact
d^{\mathcal{M}}
-dimensional
\mathrm{C}^{2\alpha}
-manifold for some
\alpha>0
.
Let
m\in\N
and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{m},s\in(0,\alpha]
,
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m},p\in(1,\infty)
and Banach spaces
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m},X
of class
\mathcal{HT}
such that there is a continuous multiplication

\cdot:X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{m}\rightarrow X.

Set
\mathcal{M}_{T}:=[0,T]\times\mathcal{M}
and let

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}^{\mathcal{M}}_{j}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{ind}(\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{j}}_{p_{j}}(\mathcal{M}_{T};X_{j}))=2s_{j}-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+2}{p_{j}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}^{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{ind}(\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s}_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{T};X))=2s-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+2}{p}.

There is a (unique) continuous extension of the multiplication operator to a multiplication operator

\cdot:\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{1}}_{p_{1}}(\mathcal{M}_{T};X_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{m}}_{p_{m}}(\mathcal{M}_{T};X_{m})\rightarrow\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s}_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{T};X),

if the following conditions are satisfied:

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\leq\min_{j=1,\ldots,m}s_{j}\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{regularity constraint})

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{p_{j}}\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{integrability constraint})

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}^{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\min_{j=1,\ldots,m}\operatorname{ind}^{\mathcal{M}}_{j},&\text{if all }\operatorname{ind}^{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\geq 0\\
\sum_{j:\operatorname{ind}^{\mathcal{M}}_{j}<0}\operatorname{ind}^{\mathcal{M}}_{j},&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{index constraint})

with a strict inequality in the index constraint if at least one of the Sobolev indices
\operatorname{ind}^{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
equals zero.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{M}
is a compact,
d^{\mathcal{M}}
-dimensional
\mathrm{C}^{2\alpha}
-manifold, we find a partition of unity
(\psi_{\nu})_{\nu=1}^{n}
of
\mathcal{M}
, where each function
\psi_{\nu}
is of class
\mathrm{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
, subordinate to a finite open (in
\R^{d}
) covering
\bigcup_{\nu=1}^{n}U_{\nu}\supset\mathcal{M}
of the manifold, and such that there exist
\mathrm{C}^{2\alpha}
-isomorphisms
\Phi_{\nu}:U_{\nu}\rightarrow V_{\nu}
such that
U_{\nu}\cap\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in U_{\nu}:\,\Phi_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})\in\R^{d^{\mathcal{M}}}\times\{\boldsymbol{0}\}\subseteq\R^{d}\}
.
Additionally, we may and will assume that not only
\psi_{\nu}\in\mathrm{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
, but also
\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}\in\mathrm{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
(this will be needed below).
By definition, (the equivalence class of) a function
u:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow E
then lies in the anisotropic Sobolev space
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\sigma}(\mathcal{M}_{T};E)
for some Banach space
E
and parameters
q\in(1,\infty)
and
\sigma\in(0,\alpha]
, if and only if for every
\nu=1,\ldots,n
the function
v_{\nu}(t,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}):=(\psi_{\nu}u)(t,\Phi_{i}^{-1}((\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{0})))
with
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in V_{\nu}^{\prime}:=\{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{d^{\mathcal{M}}}:\,(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{0})\in V_{\nu}\}
defines a function in
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\sigma}((V_{\nu}^{\prime})_{T})
, and which, therefore, can be extended by zero to a function (still denoted by
v
) in
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\sigma}((\R^{d^{\mathcal{M}}})_{T}
.

As we chose the functions
\psi_{\nu}
such that, additionally,
\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}\in\mathrm{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
, we infer that

\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\sigma}(\mathcal{M}_{T})\quad\text{with}\quad\big\|\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\sigma}}\leq C_{\nu,m,q,\sigma}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\sigma}}.

Employing Theorem
7.2
, we conclude that there exists a continuous extension of the multiplication operator to a multiplication on the full space
M^{\mathrm{fs}}:\,\mathrm{W}_{p_{1}}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{1}}(\R^{d}_{T})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{W}_{p_{m}}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{m}}(\R^{d}_{T})\rightarrow\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\R^{d}_{T})
.
We may, therefore, define a candidate for the desired extension of the multiplication operator by setting

(M^{\mathcal{M}}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}))(t,\boldsymbol{x}):=\sum_{\{\nu:\boldsymbol{x}\in U_{\nu}\}}M^{\mathrm{fs}}((\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{1})^{\ast}_{\nu},\ldots,(\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{m})^{\ast}_{\nu})(t,\Phi_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))

for
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathcal{M}_{T}
,
where we use the notation
(\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{i})^{\ast}_{\nu}(t,\Phi_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})):=(\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{i})(t,\boldsymbol{x})
for
\nu=1,\ldots,n
,
t\in[0,T]
and
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{M}
.
This defines a map
M^{\mathcal{M}}:\mathrm{W}_{p_{1}}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{1}}(\mathcal{M}_{T})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{W}_{p_{m}}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{m}}(\mathcal{M}_{T})\rightarrow\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
which is continuous thanks to
M^{\mathrm{fs}}
being continuous and continuity of the push-forward and pull-back through the maps
\Phi_{\nu}
.
It remains to check that this map actually extends the multiplication operator
\cdot:\,X_{1}\times\ldots\times X_{m}\rightarrow X
.
To this end, let us consider functions
u_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{p_{i}}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{i}}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
which, additionally, are continuous.
Then each function
(\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{i})_{\nu}^{\ast}
is continuous.
Since
M^{\mathrm{fs}}
is an extension of the pointwise multiplication for continuous functions, we infer that

\displaystyle M^{\mathrm{fs}}((\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{1})^{\ast}_{\nu},\ldots,(\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{m})^{\ast}_{\nu})(t,\Phi_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=(\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{1})^{\ast}_{\nu}(t,\Phi_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))\cdot\ldots(\psi_{\nu}^{1/m}u_{m})^{\ast}_{\nu}(t,\Phi_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\psi_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})\big(u_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{m}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\big).

Summing this identity over those
\nu=1,\ldots,n
for which
\boldsymbol{x}\in U_{\nu}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle M^{\mathcal{M}}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{\nu:\boldsymbol{x}\in U_{\nu}\}}\psi_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})\big(u_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{m}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\big)

\displaystyle=u_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{m}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\quad\text{for }(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathcal{M}_{T},

since
(\psi_{\nu})_{\nu=1}^{n}
is a partition of unity subject to the open covering
(U_{\nu})_{\nu=1}^{n}\supset\mathcal{M}
.
This shows that, for continuous functions,
M^{\mathcal{M}}
does indeed act as a pointwise multiplication.

On the other hand, uniqueness of the continuous extension follows from density of the space
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{j}}_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{T})\cap\mathrm{C}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
in
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot s_{j}}_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
.
∎

We will apply this result to the particular case where
\mathcal{M}=\Sigma=\partial\Omega
is the
\mathrm{C}^{2}
-boundary of a bounded domain
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
, which then has dimension
d^{\mathcal{M}}=d^{\Sigma}=d-1
.

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
be the boundary of a bounded
\mathrm{C}^{2}
-domain
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
and let
p\in(1,\infty)
,
K^{\Omega},K^{\Sigma}\in\N
.
Assume that one of the following cases is valid:

•

p>d
,

•

p\geq d-\frac{d+1-K^{\Omega}}{K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}}
and
p\geq\frac{d+2}{2}
and
p\geq\frac{K^{\Omega}-1}{2K^{\Omega}-1}(d+2)
,

•

p\geq d-\frac{d+1-K^{\Omega}}{K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}}
and
p\geq\frac{(K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}-1)(d+2)-K^{\Sigma}}{2(K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma})-1}
.

Then, for all Lipschitz continuous functions
\psi^{\Omega}_{1},\ldots,\psi^{\Omega}_{K^{\Omega}},\psi^{\Sigma}_{1},\ldots,\psi^{\Sigma}_{K^{\Sigma}}\in\operatorname{Lip}(\R^{N};\R)
, the function

f:(\R^{N})^{K^{\Omega}}\times(\R^{N})^{K^{\Sigma}}\rightarrow\R,\quad f(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma})=\prod_{k=1}^{K^{\Omega}}\psi^{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k})\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{K^{\Sigma}}\psi^{\Sigma}_{k}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{\Sigma})

acts as a continuous Nemytskii operator

F:\quad[\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})]^{K^{\Omega}}\times[\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{p}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})]^{K^{\Sigma}}\rightarrow\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T}),

via

[F(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma})](t,\boldsymbol{x})=f(\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{u}^{\Sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))\quad\text{for a.e.\ }(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\Sigma_{T}.

Proof.

We start with the special case
N=1
and, for the moment, assume that each function
\psi^{\Omega}_{k}=\psi_{k}^{\Sigma}\equiv 1
is the identity on
\R
, for all
k=1,\ldots,K^{\Omega}
resp.
k=1,\ldots,K^{\Sigma}
.
We may w.l.o.g. assume that
K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}\geq 2
; otherwise, the assertion is trivial in view of the anisotropic version of the Sobolev embedding theorem.
Employing Corollary
7.3
for
\alpha=1
, we check the validity of the regularity, the integrability and the index constraint:

Regularity constraint:
Obviously,
\min\{1,1-\frac{1}{2p}\}>\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p}
, thus there is nothing to check here.

Integrability constraint:
Naively, we would get the integrability constraint
\frac{K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}}{p}\leq\frac{1}{p}
, which is not valid, in general, under the imposed conditions.
However, we may use anisotropic Sobolev embeddings into spaces with lower regularity, but higher integrability parameters, provided by the continuous embeddings
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}_{p}(\Sigma_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
and
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
, where we look for integrability parameters
q,r\in(1,\infty)
such that (in the best case) the anisotropic (parabolic) Sobolev index of the spaces involved in each of these embeddings is the same.
That is, we look for
q,r\in(p,\infty)
such that

2-\frac{d+2}{p}=1-\frac{1}{p}-\frac{d+1}{q}\quad\text{and}\quad 2-\frac{d+1}{p}=1-\frac{1}{p}-\frac{d+1}{r}

which is equivalent to the condition that

(6)

1-\frac{d+1}{p}=-\frac{d+1}{q}\quad\text{and}\quad 1-\frac{d}{p}=-\frac{d+1}{r}.

The latter two equations have solutions
q,r\in(p,\infty)
if (and only if)
p<d
. However, one may see that choosing
q,r\in(p,\infty)
sufficiently large in case
p\geq d
, the index constraint will still hold true for the spaces into which we embed, so that Corollary
7.3
is still applicable.
We, therefore, focus on the critical case
p\in(1,d)
.
In this case, the parameters
q,r\in(p,\infty)
can be defined by the equalities in equation (
6
), i.e.

\frac{1}{q}=\frac{(d+1-p)^{+}}{p(d+1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{r}=\frac{(d-p)^{+}}{p(d+1)}.

For these
q,r
, the spaces
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
(which has the anisotropic Sobolev index
2-\frac{d+2}{p}
) and
\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
(which has the anisotropic Sobolev index
2-\frac{d+1}{p}
) then satisfy the integrability constraint if and only if

\frac{1}{p}\geq\frac{K^{\Omega}}{q}+\frac{K^{\Sigma}}{r}=\frac{K^{\Omega}(d+1-p)^{+}+K^{\Sigma}(d-p)^{+}}{p(d+1)},

i.e.
K^{\Omega}(d+1-p)^{+}+K^{\Sigma}(d-p)^{+}\leq d+1
.
We may, therefore, conclude that this is a sufficient condition to satisfy the integrability constraint for the existence of a continuous extension of the multiplication to a map

[\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})]^{K^{\Omega}}\times[\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})]^{K^{\Sigma}}\rightarrow\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T}).

Index constraint:

We employ the same Sobolev embeddings which we have used for the integrability constraint, i.e. the embeddings
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
and
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
.

If
p\geq d
, then
p,r\in(1,\infty)
can be chosen arbitrarily large and the Sobolev indices of
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
and
\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
, thus, can be chosen to be arbitrarily close to
1-\frac{1}{p}>0
, i.e., in particular, positive.
In this case, both these indices are strictly greater than
1-\frac{d+2}{p}
, which is the Sobolev index of
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
, and the index constraint can be satisfied.

Thus, let us consider the case
p\in(1,d)
next. Then, the Sobolev indices are not affected by the Sobolev embedding, thanks to the choice (
6
).
The index constraint is trivial in case
p\geq\frac{d+2}{2}
, since then
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{p}(\Omega_{T})
and
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{p}(\Sigma_{T})
have non-negative indices (
2-\frac{d+2}{p}\geq 0
and
2-\frac{d+1}{p}>0
) strictly greater than the index (
1-\frac{d+2}{p}
) of the space
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
.

We may, thus, focus on the case
p<\frac{d+2}{2}
.
In the case
p\in[\frac{d+1}{2},\frac{d+2}{2})
, it holds that
2-\frac{d+2}{p}<0
whereas
2-\frac{d+1}{p}\geq 0
, thus, for the index constraint to be satisfied, we have to ensure that

K^{\Omega}(2-\frac{d+2}{p})\geq 1-\frac{d+2}{p}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad p\geq\frac{(K^{\Omega}-1)^{+}}{(2K^{\Omega}-1)^{+}}(d+2).

As the right-hand side tends to
\frac{d+2}{2}>\frac{d+1}{2}
as
K^{\Omega}\rightarrow\infty
, this, in general, constitutes an additional constraint on
p
.

If
p<\frac{d+1}{2}
, both
2-\frac{d+2}{2}
and
2-\frac{d+1}{2}
are strictly negative, thus, we need to ensure that

\displaystyle K^{\Omega}(2-\frac{d+2}{p})+K^{\Sigma}(2-\frac{d+1}{p})\geq 1-\frac{d+2}{p}

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad p\geq\frac{(K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}-1)(d+2)-K^{\Sigma}}{2(K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma})-1}.

To finish the proof, the case of general Lipschitz continuous functions
\psi_{i}
and
\psi_{i}^{\Sigma}
follows from the observation that for all
\sigma\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
any Lipschitz continuous function
\psi
continuously maps functions
u
in
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot\sigma}
to functions in
\psi\circ u\in\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot\sigma}
, thus we may interpret functions in
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{p}(\Omega_{T})
and
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T})
by the continuous embedding from above as functions in
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}_{q}(\Sigma_{T})
and
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}_{r}(\Sigma_{T})
, respectively, then apply the Nemytskii operator corresponding to the functions
\psi_{i}
and
\psi_{i}^{\Sigma}
, respectively, and finally use the previous considerations to deduce the general assertion from the special case
\psi_{i}=\psi_{i}^{\Sigma}\equiv 1
.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

Note that the particular case
p>d
covered in
[
50
]
is also admissible in our situation, but we may actually allow for lower integrability index
p\in(1,\infty)
, provided the exponents
K^{\Omega}
and
K^{\Sigma}
for the polynomial growth bounds are not too large.

In fact, for the standard multicomponent Langmuir sorption model, we can choose
K^{\Omega}=K^{\Sigma}=1
, hence, all
p\geq\frac{d+2}{2}
become admissible for Corollary
7.4
.

To include an additional bounded and Lipschitz continuous prefactor, let us note the following special case of an extension lemma for the multiplication:

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\R^{d}
be a compact, regular Riemannian manifold of dimension
d^{\mathcal{M}}
and let
T>0
and
p\in(\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+3}{2},\infty)
be given.
Moreover, let functions
\varphi\in\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{T})\cap\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
and
f\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
be given.
Then the function
f\varphi
belongs to the class
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
as well and there is a constant
C>0
independent of
\varphi
and
f
such that

\big\|f\varphi\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}}\leq C\big\|\varphi\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}\cap\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}}\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}}

for all
\varphi\in\mathrm{L}_{\infty}\cap\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
and
f\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
.
In other words, the bilinear form

(\mathrm{L}_{\infty}\cap\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})})(\mathcal{M}_{T})\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})\ni(\varphi,f)\mapsto\varphi f\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})

is continuous.

Remark 7.7
.

Note that this result is not just a direct consequence of Amann’s multiplication lemma, since the integrability condition is not satisfied.
However, using anisotropic versions of the Sobolev embedding theorems, we are able to demonstrate the result.

Also note that we obtain a function
\varphi\in\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{T})\cap\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
, if we plug in a function
g\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T};\R^{m})
into a bounded and Lipschitz continuous function
\Phi\in\mathrm{W}_{\infty}^{1}(\R^{m};\R)
, i.e. consider
\varphi:=\Phi\circ g
.

Proof of Lemma
7.6
.

Obviously,
\varphi f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{T})
since

\big\|\varphi f\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}\leq\big\|\varphi\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}.

Let us set
\sigma:=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
To estimate the seminorms
\big[\varphi f\big]_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(\sigma,0)}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}
and
\big[\varphi f\big]_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(0,2\sigma)}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}
, we use the anisotropic Sobolev embeddings

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}_{q,p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{q^{\ast},\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{T}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}_{p,r}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{\infty,r^{\ast}}(\mathcal{M}_{T}),

which are valid if we can simultaneously satisfy the conditions

\displaystyle 2-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+3}{p}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+1}{p}-\frac{2}{q}

and

\displaystyle 1-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+3}{p}

\displaystyle>-\frac{2}{q^{\ast}},

\displaystyle 2-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+3}{p}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{3}{p}-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}}{r}

and

\displaystyle 1-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+3}{p}

\displaystyle>-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}}{r^{\ast}},

and where
q,q^{\ast},r,r^{\ast}\in(p,\infty)
are chosen such that
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\ast}}=\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{r^{\ast}}
.
By summing up the conditions on
q
and
q^{\ast}
, and on
r
and
r^{\ast}
, respectively, we observe that such
q
and
q^{\ast}
can be chosen if and only if

2-\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+3}{p}>0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad p>\frac{d^{\mathcal{M}}+3}{2}.

For the seminorm
\big[\varphi f\big]_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(\sigma,0)}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}
we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\big[\varphi f\big]_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(\sigma,0)}}^{p}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\big\|(\varphi f)(s,\cdot)-(\varphi f)(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\mathcal{M})}^{p}}{\big|s-t\big|^{\sigma p+1}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\big\|\varphi(s,\cdot)(f(s,\cdot)-f(t,\cdot))\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\mathcal{M})}^{p}+\big\|(\varphi(s,\cdot)-\varphi(t,\cdot))f(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\mathcal{M})}^{p}}{\big|s-t\big|^{\sigma p+1}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\big\|\varphi(s,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}}^{p}\big\|f(s,\cdot)-f(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\mathcal{M})}^{p}+\big\|\varphi(s,\cdot)-\varphi(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{r}(\mathcal{M})}^{p}\big\|f(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{r^{\ast}}(\mathcal{M})}^{p}}{\big|s-t\big|^{\sigma p+1}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\left(\big\|\varphi\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}^{p}\big[f\big]_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(\sigma,0)}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}^{p}+\big\|\varphi\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p,r}^{(\sigma,0)}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}^{p}\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty,r^{\ast}}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}^{p}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|\varphi\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})\cap\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}\right)^{p}.

Similarly, we may estimate

\displaystyle\big[\varphi f\big]_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(0,2\sigma)}}^{p}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\frac{\big\|(\varphi f)(\cdot,\boldsymbol{x})-(\varphi f)(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T)}^{p}}{\big|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\big|^{2\sigma p+d}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{x})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\frac{\big\|\varphi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{x})(f(\cdot,\boldsymbol{x})-f(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y}))\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T)}^{p}+\big\|(\varphi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{x})-\varphi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y}))f(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T)}^{p}}{\big|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\big|^{2\sigma p+d}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{x})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\frac{1}{\big|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\big|^{2\sigma p+d}}\cdot\big(\big\|\varphi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{x})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(0,T)}^{p}\big\|f(\cdot,\boldsymbol{x})-f(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(0,T)}^{p}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\|\varphi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{x})-\varphi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(0,T)}^{p}\big\|f(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\ast}}(0,T)}^{p}\big)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{x})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|\varphi\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2p})}\cap\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\mathcal{M}_{T})}\right)^{p}.

∎

Therefore, we may conclude that in this case, the reference function
\boldsymbol{r}^{\ast}
for the sorption rates defined in equation (
5
) lies in the Neumann trace space
\mathbb{G}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
.
Let us denote by
(\boldsymbol{c}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,\ast})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
the unique solution of the quasi-autonomous linear initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T,i}(c_{i}^{\ast},c_{i}^{\Sigma,\ast})

\displaystyle=(0,0,r_{i}^{\ast})

\displaystyle(c_{i}(0,\cdot),c_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot))

\displaystyle=(c^{0}_{i},c^{\Sigma,0}_{i}),

which will take the role of reference functions.
We write
c_{i}=\bar{c}_{i}+c_{i}^{\ast}
and
c_{i}^{\Sigma}=\bar{c}_{i}^{\Sigma}+c_{i}^{\Sigma,\ast}
and observe that the functions
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
are solutions of the reaction-diffusion-advection-sorption model under consideration if and only if the remainder functions
(\boldsymbol{\bar{c}},\boldsymbol{\bar{c}^{\Sigma}})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
solve the reduced semilinear
zero time trace problem

\mathcal{L}^{0}_{T,i}(\bar{c}_{i},\bar{c}_{i}^{\Sigma})=\mathcal{N}_{T,i}(\bar{c}_{i}+c_{i}^{\ast},\bar{c}_{i}^{\Sigma}+c_{i}^{\Sigma,\ast})-(0,0,r_{i}^{\ast}),

where
\mathcal{L}^{0}_{T}:=\mathcal{L}_{T}|_{\mathring{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathring{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)}
is the restriction of the linear parabolic operator
\mathcal{L}_{T}
to zero time trace functions from the class

\mathring{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathring{\mathbb{E}}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T):=\{(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T):\,(\boldsymbol{c}(0,\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot))=(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})\}.

From here on, we may use
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity and Banach’s Contraction Mapping Theorem as powerful tools to establish local-in-time existence and uniqueness of strong
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
-solutions to (
RDASS
) and their continuous dependence on the initial data.

Theorem 7.8

(Local-in time well-posedness)
.

Let
J=(0,T^{\prime})\subseteq\R
for some
T^{\prime}>0
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Assume that assumptions (

A
advec

), (

A
F

sorp

), (

A
M

sorp

), (

A
B

sorp

), (

A
F

ch

), (

A
N

ch

), (

A
P

ch

) and (

A
S

sorp

) are satisfied, and
p\in[\frac{d+2}{2},\infty)
satisfies one of the following conditions:

•

p>d
,

•

p\geq d-\frac{d+1-K^{\Omega}}{K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}}
and
p\geq\frac{d+2}{2}
and
p\geq\frac{K^{\Omega}-1}{2K^{\Omega}-1}(d+2)
,

•

p\geq d-\frac{d+1-K^{\Omega}}{K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma}}
and
p\geq\frac{d+2}{2}-\frac{d+3}{4(K^{\Omega}+K^{\Sigma})-2}
.

Then, for each initial data in the class

c^{0}_{i}\in\mathrm{W}^{2-\nicefrac{{2}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Omega),\quad c^{\Sigma,0}_{i}\in\mathrm{W}^{2-\nicefrac{{2}}{{p}}}_{p}(\Sigma)\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,N,

which in case
p>3
additionally satisfy the compatibility conditions

-d_{i}\partial_{\nu}c^{0}_{i}=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c^{0}_{i},c^{\Sigma,0}_{i})\quad\text{on }\Sigma\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,N,

there are
T\in(0,T^{\prime})
and a unique local-in time strong solution
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
of system (
RDASS
).
The solution depends continuously w.r.t.
\big\|\cdot\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}}
on the initial data and can be extended in a unique way to a maximal-in-time solution on some time interval
(0,T_{\mathrm{max}})
with
T_{\mathrm{max}}\in(0,\infty]
.
If, additionally,
c^{0}_{i}\geq 0
(a.e.),
c^{\Sigma,0}_{i}\geq 0
(
\sigma
-a.e.) for all components
i=1,\ldots,N
, then
c_{i}\geq 0
a.e. on
\Omega_{T}
and
c_{i}^{\Sigma}\geq 0

\sigma
-a.e. on
\Sigma_{T}
, for all
i=1,\ldots,N
.

Proof.

We employ the theory of
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity and Banach’s Contraction Mapping Principle.
Let us fix some initial data
(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Omega}\times\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Sigma}
and choose positive numbers
\rho_{0}>0
and
\tau_{0}\in(0,T^{\prime})
.
For each
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
and
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
, we define a nonlinear map
\Phi=\Phi_{\rho,\tau}:D_{\rho,\tau}\subseteq\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(\tau)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(\tau)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(\tau)
on the complete metric space (with the metric inherited from
\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(\tau)
)

\displaystyle D_{\rho,\tau}

\displaystyle=\{(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(\tau)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(\tau):\,(\boldsymbol{c}(0,\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot))=(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0}),

\displaystyle\qquad\big\|(\boldsymbol{c}-\mathrm{e}^{tA}\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\mathrm{e}^{tA^{\Sigma}}\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\big\|_{\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(\tau)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(\tau)}\leq\rho\}.

Given
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in D_{\rho,\tau}
, let
\Phi(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}):=(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma})
be the unique solution to the quasi-autonomous, linear initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla-\boldsymbol{D}\Delta)\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\boldsymbol{D}\Delta_{\Sigma})\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})+\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{D}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\boldsymbol{v}|_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{c}^{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma.

By
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-optimal regularity in Lemma
4.1
, this problem has a unique solution in the class
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(\tau)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(\tau)
if and only if

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)^{N}\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Sigma)^{N},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau};\R^{N}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})+\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau};\R^{N}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1/2-1/2p)}(\Sigma_{\tau})

and, additionally, if
p>3
, the compatibility condition

\boldsymbol{s}^{0}:=\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})=-\boldsymbol{D}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\partial\Omega}

in the trace sense is satisfied.
We recall that
A=-\boldsymbol{D}\Delta
is defined on the Neumann domain
\dom(A)=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Omega;\R^{N}):\,-\boldsymbol{D}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0}\text{ on }\partial\Omega\}
and
A^{\Sigma}=-\boldsymbol{D}^{\Sigma}\Delta_{\Sigma}
on
\dom(A^{\Sigma})=\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Sigma;\R^{N})
.
To estimate the norm of
\boldsymbol{v}-\mathrm{e}^{tA}\boldsymbol{c}^{0}
, we set

\boldsymbol{s}^{\ast}(t,\cdot):=\mathrm{e}^{tA^{\Sigma}}\boldsymbol{s}^{0}\quad\text{for }t\geq 0,

which defines a function
\boldsymbol{s}^{\ast}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-1/2p)}(\Sigma_{\tau})
, and let
\boldsymbol{c}^{\ast}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
be the unique strong
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
-solution to the inhomogeneous initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla-\boldsymbol{D}\Delta)\boldsymbol{c}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0})\quad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{D}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\boldsymbol{c}^{\ast}|_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{s}^{\ast}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{c}^{\ast}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{c}^{0}\quad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

Then the remainder
\boldsymbol{w}:=\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{c}^{\ast}
solves the initial-boundary value problem with zero initial data

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla-\boldsymbol{D}\Delta)\boldsymbol{w}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0})\quad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{D}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\boldsymbol{w}|_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{s}^{\ast}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0}\quad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

where (in case
p>3
) the boundary and initial data are compatible thanks to

\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})|_{t=0}=\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})=\boldsymbol{s}^{0}=\boldsymbol{s}^{\ast}|_{t=0}.

Thus, given
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
and
(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\in D_{\rho,\tau}
and corresponding solutions
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma})=\Phi(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
and
(\boldsymbol{\tilde{v}},\boldsymbol{\tilde{v}}^{\Sigma})=\Phi(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})
, the difference
\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{v}}
may be written as
\boldsymbol{w}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}
, where

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla-\boldsymbol{D}\Delta)(\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{w}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}})\quad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{D}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{w}})|_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\quad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{w}})(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0}\quad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

Since the latter two systems have
zero initial data
, we may again employ Lemma
4.1
, which gives us a maximal regularity constant
C=C(\tau_{0})>0
, independent of
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
such that for all
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
the estimates

\big\|\boldsymbol{w}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{s}^{\ast}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1/2-1/2p)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)

and

\big\|\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{w}}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1/2-1/2p)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)

are valid.
We observe that the maps
D_{\rho,\tau}\ni(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\mapsto\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})
and
D_{\rho,\tau}\ni(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\mapsto\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(1/2-1/2p)}(\Sigma_{\tau})
are both Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constants which are uniformly bounded for
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
, and which tend to zero as
\tau\rightarrow 0
uniformly for all
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
, e.g.

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle=\big\|\int_{0}^{1}(\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}})\cdot(\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega})^{\prime}(s\boldsymbol{c}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}})\,\mathrm{d}s\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{\tau})}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\tau^{1-\frac{1}{p\gamma^{\Omega}}}+\big\|s\boldsymbol{c}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{\gamma^{\Omega}-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big(\tau^{1-\frac{1}{p\gamma^{\Omega}}}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}-1}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{\gamma^{\Omega}}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{\gamma^{\Omega}-1}\big).

Let us note that

\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{\tau})

embeds continuously if
p\geq\frac{(\gamma^{\Omega}-1)\cdot(d+2)}{2\gamma^{\Omega}}
, and the embedding constant may be chosen uniform for all
\tau\in(0,T^{\prime})
.
Moreover, if
p>\frac{(\gamma^{\Omega}-1)(d+2)}{2\gamma^{\Omega}}
, the embedding constant tends to zero as
\tau\rightarrow 0+
.
(Recall that
(\boldsymbol{c}(0,\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot))=(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})
for all
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in D_{\rho,T}
, thus
\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\in\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
.)
Similarly,

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big(\tau^{1-\frac{1}{p\gamma^{\Omega}}}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}-1}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{\gamma^{\Sigma}}+\big\|\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Sigma}}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{\gamma^{\Sigma}-1}\big)

provided
p\geq\frac{\gamma^{\Sigma}-1}{\gamma^{\Sigma}}\cdot\frac{d+1}{2}
.
Note that both
p>\frac{(\gamma^{\Omega}-1)(d+2)}{2\gamma^{\Omega}}
and
p>\frac{(\gamma^{\Sigma}-1)(d+1)}{2\gamma^{\Sigma}}
are always satisfied, if
p\geq\frac{d+2}{2}
.
This means that there are constants
C_{\tau,\rho}>0
, which can be chosen uniformly bounded for
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
and
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
, and tend to zero as
\tau\rightarrow 0+
, uniformly for all
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
, such that

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{w}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tau,\rho}\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+1\big)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{v}}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}=\big\|\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{w}}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tau,\rho}\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big),

see Lemma
7.9
below.
On the other hand, concerning the terms on the boundary, we may similarly consider a reference function
\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,\ast}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{N}
defined as the solution to the quasi-autonomous surface diffusion system with source terms

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\boldsymbol{D}^{\Sigma}\Delta_{\Sigma})\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,\ast}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})+\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma.

Then
\boldsymbol{w}^{\Sigma}:=\boldsymbol{v}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,\ast}
must solve the initial value problem on the surface

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\boldsymbol{D}^{\Sigma}\Delta_{\Sigma})\boldsymbol{w}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})-\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0})+\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma.

For this quasi-autonomous linear system, similar to the situation in the bulk phase, we may derive estimates of the form

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tau,\rho}\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega)}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+1\big),

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{w}}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tau,\rho}\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{c}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big),

where again the constants
C_{\rho,\tau}>0
may be chosen uniformly bounded for
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
, and such that they tend to zero as
\tau\rightarrow 0+
, uniformly for
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
.
Putting these estimates together, we infer that for sufficiently small
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
, the map
\Phi
maps
D_{\rho,\tau}
into
D_{\rho,\tau}
and is a strict contraction.
Hence, by Banach’s contraction mapping principle,
\Phi
has a unique fixed point
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in D_{\rho,\tau}
, which then is the unique solution to the semilinear reaction-diffusion-sorption problem in the subclass
D_{\rho,\tau}\subseteq\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(\tau)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(\tau)
for all sufficiently small
\tau\in(0,\tau_{1}]
and
\rho\in(0,\rho_{1}]
.
We may conclude uniqueness of the solution in the class
\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(\tau)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(\tau)
, using a contradiction argument and the observation that by above reasoning any two solutions will coincide at least on some small, but positive time interval:
In fact, given two solutions
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
and
(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)\hookrightarrow\BUC([0,T];\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)^{N}\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Sigma)^{N})
, set

t_{0}:=\sup\{\tau\in[0,T]:\,(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})|_{t\in[0,\tau]}=(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})|_{t\in[0,\tau]}\},

where
t_{0}>0
follows from the previous considerations (choose
\tau>0
small enough, such that
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})|_{t\in[0,\tau]},(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma})|_{t\in[0,\tau]}\in D_{\rho,\tau}
).
Now, assume
t_{0}<T
.
Then, by continuity w.r.t. the
\big\|\cdot\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}}
-norm, we find that

(\boldsymbol{c}(t_{0},\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t_{0},\cdot))=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t_{0}}(\boldsymbol{c}(\tau,\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(\tau,\cdot))=(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}(t_{0},\cdot),\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma}(t_{0},\cdot)).

By time-invariance of the problem, we then find a
unique
(local-in time) solution to the problem for given initial data
(\boldsymbol{c}(t_{0},\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t_{0},\cdot))
, hence
(\boldsymbol{c}(\tau,\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(\tau,\cdot))|_{\tau\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\varepsilon]}=(\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}(\tau,\cdot),\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\Sigma}(\tau,\cdot))|_{\tau\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\varepsilon]}
for some
\varepsilon>0
, contradicting the choice of
t_{0}
!

The extension to a maximal-in-time solution can be achieved based on the standard technique employing local-in-time existence and uniqueness of strong solutions and Zorn’s lemma.

Continuous dependence on the initial data is, essentially, a consequence of the inverse function theorem:
As the terms
\boldsymbol{s}^{\ast}
,
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0})
,
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})
and
\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})
depend continuously on the initial data, so do the reference functions
\boldsymbol{c}^{\ast}
and
\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,\ast}
as well as the remainder parts
(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{w}^{\Sigma})
, hence, ultimatively the solution
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
itself.
∎

In the proof of the local-in-time well-posedness result, we employed the following auxiliary result which tells us how the constant in the anisotropic embedding theorem depends on the time horizon
\tau>0
.

Lemma 7.9

(
\tau
-dependence of the Sobolev embedding constant)
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
be a domain with the extension property.
Moreover, let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
such that
2-\frac{d+2}{p}\geq-\frac{d+2}{q}
.
Then, for all
\tau\in(0,\infty)
, the space
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
continuously embeds into
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})
.
Moreover, for functions in the subclass

\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau}):=\{u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau}):\,u|_{t=0}=0\text{ in the trace sense}\}

we find that

\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}}:=\big\|\partial_{t}u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}}+\big\|\nabla^{2}u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}}

is an equivalent norm, for which we find a
\tau
-independent constant
C=C(p,q,\Omega)
such that

\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}}\leq C\tau^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}+\frac{2}{d+2}}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}}\quad\text{for all }\tau>0,\,u\in\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau}).

Proof.

Let us first consider the case

2-\frac{d+2}{p}=-\frac{d+2}{q}.

Continuity of the embedding then follows from the anisotropic version of the Sobolev embedding theorem.
Let
\tau>0
be given and set
\lambda:=\sqrt{\tau}>0
.
Denote by
\mathcal{E}
an extension operator, which is both continuous from
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)
to
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{d})
and from
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Omega)
to
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\R^{d})
.
For any function
u\in\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
, we denote by
U\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\R\times\R^{d})
the extension of
U
defined as

U(t,\boldsymbol{x}):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }t<0\text{ or }t>2T,\\
\mathcal{E}u(t,\boldsymbol{x}),&\text{if }t\in[0,T],\\
\mathcal{E}u(2T-t,\boldsymbol{x}),&\text{if }t\in(T,2T].\end{cases}

Further, let us introduce

\tilde{U}(s,\boldsymbol{y}):=U(\lambda^{2}s,\lambda\boldsymbol{y}),\quad s\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{y}\in\R^{d}.

This defines functions
U,\tilde{U}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\R\times\R^{d})
and by the scaling we have ensured that
\tilde{U}
has support in
[0,2]\times\R^{d}
.
We may estimate the
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-norm of
u
as follows

\displaystyle\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq\big\|U\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R\times\R^{d})}=\left(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|U(t,\boldsymbol{x})\big|^{q}\,\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|\tilde{U}(\tfrac{t}{\lambda^{2}},\tfrac{\boldsymbol{x}}{\lambda})\big|^{q}\,\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\right)^{1/q}=\lambda^{\frac{d+2}{q}}\left(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|\tilde{U}(s,\boldsymbol{y})\big|^{q}\,\,\mathrm{d}(s,\boldsymbol{y})\right)^{1/q}=\lambda^{\frac{d+2}{q}}\big\|\tilde{U}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{d+1})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\lambda^{\frac{d+2}{q}}\big\|\tilde{U}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\R\times\R^{d})}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\lambda^{\frac{d+2}{q}}\big\{\big(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|\partial_{s}\tilde{U}(s,\boldsymbol{y})\big|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}(s,\boldsymbol{y})\big)^{1/p}+\big(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{2}\tilde{U}(s,\boldsymbol{y})\big|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}(s,\boldsymbol{y})\big)^{1/p}\big\}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\lambda^{\frac{d+2}{q}}\cdot\lambda^{2}\big\{\big(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|\partial_{t}U(\lambda^{2}s,\lambda\boldsymbol{y})\big|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}(s,\boldsymbol{y})\big)^{1/p}+\big(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{2}U)(\lambda^{2}s,\lambda\boldsymbol{y})\big|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}(s,\boldsymbol{y})\big)^{1/p}\big\}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\lambda^{\frac{d+2}{q}}\cdot\lambda^{2}\cdot\lambda^{-\frac{d+2}{p}}\big\{\big(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|\partial_{t}U(t,\boldsymbol{x})\big|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\big)^{1/p}+\big(\int_{\R^{d+1}}\big|(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{2}U)(t,\boldsymbol{x})\big|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\big)^{1/p}\big\}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\tau^{1-\frac{d+2}{2p}+\frac{d+2}{2q}}\big\|U\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}}\leq C^{\prime}\tau^{1-\frac{d+2}{2p}+\frac{d+2}{2q}}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}}=C^{\prime}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}}.

For the general case
2-\frac{d+2}{p}>-\frac{d+2}{q}
, we choose
q^{\ast}\in(q,\infty)
and
p^{\ast}\in(1,p)
such that

2-\frac{d+2}{p^{\ast}}=-\frac{d+2}{q^{\ast}}.

As we have just seen, then

\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\ast}}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C^{\prime}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p^{\ast}}^{(1,2)}}.

Thus, choosing
r,s\in(1,\infty]
such that
\frac{1}{r}:=\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{q^{\ast}}
and
\frac{1}{s}=\frac{1}{p^{\ast}}-\frac{1}{p}
, we find by Hölder’s inequality that

\displaystyle\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\ast}}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big\|1\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{r}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}(\big|\Omega\big|\cdot\tau)^{1/r}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p^{\ast}}^{(1,2)}}\leq C^{\prime}(\big|\Omega\big|\cdot\tau)^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}+\frac{2}{d+2}}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}}\big\|1\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{s}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}(\big|\Omega\big|\cdot\tau)^{1/r+1/r}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}}=C^{\prime}(\big|\Omega\big|\cdot\tau)^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}-\frac{2}{d+2}}\big\|u\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)}}.

∎

8.
Auxiliary estimates for parabolic bulk-surface systems based on the duality method

Before we prove our global-in-time existence result, we provide some preliminary results, which we later on will employ to establish global-in time existence of strong solutions.
Some of these results are well-known and have already proved very useful for reaction-diffusion-systems with, say, homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions.
Others can be seen as a generalisation of auxiliary results in
[
23
]
by which we remove some of the restrictions imposed there, especially when passing from duality-method based estimates in the bulk phase to estimates for the corresponding boundary trace terms.

We start with the comparison principle, which for the pure bulk case, is a well-established tool for proving global-in-time existence for parabolic equations, in particular for reaction-diffusion-systems with a triangular structure, cf. M. Pierre’s survey
[
52
]
.

Lemma 8.1

(Positivity principle)
.

Let a time horizon
T>0
, parameters
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\alpha,\beta>0
, and strictly positive
d\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
,
d^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\Sigma)
be given.

(1)

Let
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{T})
,
g^{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{T})
,
v_{0}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)
with
-\beta\partial_{\nu}v_{0}=g^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)
(on
\Sigma
and in the trace sense) if
p>3
.
If
v\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T})
is a strong
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
–solution of the initial boundary value problem

\displaystyle\alpha\partial_{t}v+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v-\beta\dv(d\nabla v)

\displaystyle=f\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Omega_{T},

\displaystyle-\beta\partial_{\nu}v

\displaystyle=g^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

\displaystyle v(0)

\displaystyle=v_{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

where
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{U}_{\tilde{p}}^{\Omega}(T)
with
\tilde{p}>d+2
, then
f,v_{0}\geq 0
and
g^{\Sigma}\leq 0
imply
v\geq 0
.

(2)

Let
f^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{T})
,
v_{0}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Sigma)
.
If
v^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T})
is a strong solution of the initial value problem

\displaystyle\alpha\partial_{t}v^{\Sigma}-\beta\dv_{\Sigma}(d^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=f^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

\displaystyle v^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=v_{0}^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma,

where
f^{\Sigma},v_{0}^{\Sigma}\geq 0
, then
v^{\Sigma}\geq 0
.

Proof.

This result can be established analogously to
[
23
, Lemma 5.2]
.
∎

The next auxiliary result may be seen as a slightly generalised version of
[
23
, Lemma 6.2]
.
The main difference is that we do not have to restrict to the cases
p,q\geq 2
, but all
p,q>1
are allowed.
To establish the result, we combine classical duality techniques, cf. M. Pierre’s survey
[
52
]
, with interpolation methods.

Lemma 8.2

(Dual estimate)
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty]
,
T>0
and
\alpha,\beta>0
be fixed, and let
\tau\in(0,T]
.
Assume that
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{U}_{\tilde{p}}^{\Omega}(T)
with
\tilde{p}>d+2
.
Let
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})
,
g^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{\tau})
and
v_{0}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)
be given, and assume that the compatibility condition
-\beta\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v_{0}=g^{\Sigma}(0)
is satisfied if
p>3
.
Then there is a constant
C=C(q,T)>0
such that for every strong solution
v\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
of the initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle\alpha\partial_{t}v+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v-\beta\dv(d\nabla v)

\displaystyle=f\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Omega_{\tau},

(7)

\displaystyle-\beta\partial_{\nu}v

\displaystyle=g^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},

\displaystyle v(0)

\displaystyle=v_{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

the following estimate is valid:

\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|v(\tau,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|g^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|v_{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)}\right).

Remark 8.3
.

Note that, in particular, the constant
C>0
does neither depend on the data
f
,
v_{0}
and
g^{\Sigma}
, nor on the time horizon
\tau\in(0,T]
.

For the case
p,q\in[2,\infty]
, a quite similar result has been derived in
[
23
, Lemma 6.2]
(originally, for cylindrical domains, but the proof easily extends to general
\mathrm{C}^{2-}
-domains). We employ the following strategy: For
p>\frac{d+2}{2}
, i.e. the space
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}((0,T)\times\Omega)
continuously embeds into the space of continuous functions on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, we first consider the case
q\geq 2
and distinguish the three subcases
q=2
,
q=\infty
and
q\in(2,\infty)
:
Here, the Hilbert space case
q=2
can be handled by multiplying the first line of (
7
) by
v
, integrating over
\Omega
and using integration by parts. This gives an estimate on the bulk term
\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Omega_{\tau})}
. The boundary term
\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Sigma_{\tau})}
can be estimated by employing trace results on Bessel potential spaces
\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{s-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\Sigma)
for
s\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2})
, combined with an interpolation inequality for
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and the estimate established for the bulk term.
The second case
q=\infty
is easier and can be handled using the estimate
\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\Omega_{\tau})}
, which is trivial for continuous functions
u
.
By the Riesz–Thorin Interpolation Theorem, we refer to e.g.
[
48
, Theorem 2.6]
for a version which is general enough to be applicable here, the estimates for intermediate interpolation parameters
q\in(2,\infty)
then follow as well.

In the case
p\leq\frac{d+2}{2}
we may approximate the initial and boundary data and the source terms by regularised data, so that the results for the case
p>\frac{d+2}{2}
become applicable.

On the other hand, in several previous works on bulk reaction-diffusion-advection systems, dual estimates have been heavily used as a tool to establish global-in-time existence for particular classes of reaction-diffusion-advection systems, cf., e.g.,
[
52
, Lemma 3.4]
.
To estimate the bulk term
\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}
for the range
q\in(1,\infty)
via the
duality method
, we proceed as follows: We multiply the evolutionary PDE by a function
\theta
, which is chosen in such a way that it solves an evolutionary problem which is
dual
to the original initial-boundary value problem.
(In particular, it evolves
backwards in time
and is subject to
homogeneous boundary conditions
.)
This method relies on the fact that the dual space
(\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau}))^{\ast}
of the Lebesgue space
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})
is (up to an isometric isomorphism) the Lebesgue space
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})
for the Hölder dual exponent
q^{\prime}\in(1,\infty)
which is given by
\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}:=1-\frac{1}{q}
, whenever
q\in(1,\infty)
belongs to the reflexive range.

Combining these methods, we may allow for
p,q
from the full reflexive range
(1,\infty)
: First, we employ the duality method to get the estimate on the bulk term. Thereafter, we adjust the method used in
[
23
, Lemma 6.2]
to cover the general case
q\in(1,\infty)
.

Proof.

The case
q=\infty
can be handled exactly as in the proof of
[
23
, Lemma 6.2.]
.
Moreover, with the techniques used in the proof of
[
52
, Lemma 3.4]
and for
q\in(2,\infty)
, one may easily establish the (weaker) estimate
\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C_{q,T}\big(\big\|v^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)}+\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|g^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)
for every
q\in(1,\infty)
and
\tau\in(0,T]
.
We, therefore, focus on the boundary term
\big\|v|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{T})}
and show that it can be estimated by the right-hand side as well.
To this end, for the moment, we will additionally assume that
v\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
, e.g. by approximating
f
,
g^{\Sigma}
and
v_{0}
by smooth data, and multiply the first line in (
7
) by
v\big|v\big|^{q-2}:=\sign(v)\big|v\big|^{q-1}\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})
(which, up to a scalar factor and identification of the dual space
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}
with
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})
, is the unique element in the duality set of
v\in\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})
), then integrate over
\Omega_{\tau}
and use equation (
RDASS
) and integration by parts, to calculate that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}fv\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big(\partial_{t}v+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v-\beta\dv(d\nabla v)\big)v\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big(\frac{1}{q}\partial_{t}\big|v\big|^{q}+\frac{1}{q}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\big|v\big|^{q}\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\beta\dv(d\nabla v)\cdot v\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{q}\int_{\Omega}\big|v\big|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right]_{t=0}^{t=\tau}-\frac{1}{q}\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\dv\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\big|v\big|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\frac{1}{q}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})\big|v\big|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\beta d\nabla v\cdot\nabla(v\cdot\big|v\big|^{q-2})\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\beta\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v\cdot v\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}\big[\big\|v(\tau,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}-\big\|v(0,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}\big]+\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\beta d(q-1)\big|\nabla v\big|^{2}\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g^{\Sigma}\,v\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})),

provided these integrals exist.
The latter will follow for general
q\in(1,\infty)
from Lemma
8.4
below.
Hence, after rearrangement of the terms, we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\big\|v(\tau,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}+\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\beta d(q-1)\big|\nabla v\big|^{2}\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}\big\|v(0,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}+\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}fv\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g^{\Sigma}\,v\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{q}\big\|v(0,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}+\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big|f\big|\big|v\big|^{q-1}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big|g^{\Sigma}\big|\big|v\big|^{q-1}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})).

The desired estimate follows from this, if we can ensure that for every
\varepsilon>0
there is
C=C(q,\varepsilon)>0
(independent of
v
) such that

(8)

\varepsilon\big\|v|_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma)}^{q}\leq\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big|\nabla v\big|^{2}\big|v\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+C(q,\varepsilon)\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{q},

as we may then employ the generalized Hölder inequality to establish

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big|f\big|\big|v\big|^{q-1}\,\mathrm{d}(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{q-1},

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big|g^{\Sigma}\big|\big|v\big|^{q-1}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(x))

\displaystyle\leq\big\|g^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{q-1}

and the statement follows by Young’s inequality for products.
To get estimate (
8
), we may employ the subsequent Lemma
8.4
.

Finally, since the constant
C>0
is independent of the particular choice of the data, by approximation these estimates are also valid for less smooth data
(f,g^{\Sigma},v_{0})
, i.e. the estimate is valid for all such strong
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
-solutions.
∎

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
q\in(1,\infty)
,
\tau>0
and
\Omega\subsetneq\R^{d}
be a bounded
\mathrm{C}^{1,1}
-domain with boundary
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
.
Denote by
\cdot|_{\Sigma}:\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})
the (continuous) spatial boundary trace operator.
Then, for every
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, there is a constant
C=C(q,s,\Omega)>0
such that

\displaystyle\big\|v|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{q}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\big|v\big|^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}((0,\tau);\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega))}^{2s}\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{(1-s)q}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\big(\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big|v\big|^{q-2}\big|\nabla v\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\big)^{1/2}+\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{q/2}\big)^{2s}\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{(1-s)q},\quad v\in\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau}).

Proof.

As a preliminary step, we will derive the following identity, which is valid for every
u\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\overline{\Omega})
:

(9)

\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\Delta u\cdot u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})=-\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\big(u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}u\cdot u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})).

This identity does not immediately follow from the Gauß divergence theorem, since for
q\in(1,2)
the term
u\big|u\big|^{q-2}
will in general
not
be classically differentiable, nor can we ensure that its weak derivative lies in the space
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})
.
Therefore, we will approximate the integrands by a regularised version and, instead of
\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla(u\big|u\big|^{q-2})\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
, we consider the integral

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\big(u(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big|\nabla u\big|^{2}(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\big[1+(q-2)\big|u\big|^{2}(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{-1}\big]\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x}).

The terms in the integrand on the right-hand side are all positive for each
\varepsilon>0
and decrease in
\varepsilon>0
, thus, for the critical case
q\in(1,2)
we may use the Beppo–Levi theorem on montone convergence to conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big|\nabla u\big|^{2}(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\big[1+(q-2)\big|u\big|^{2}(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{-1}\big]\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\qquad\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla(u\big|u\big|^{q-2})\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,\infty].

For the regularised integral, we may use the Gauß divergence theorem, and obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\big(u(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\Delta u\cdot u(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}u\cdot u(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})).

Since we may estimate

\displaystyle\big\|u(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{q^{\prime}}=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\big(\frac{\big|u\big|}{\sqrt{u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}}\big)^{q^{\prime}}(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

and, using that
q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{q-1}
, so that
\frac{q-1}{2}\cdot q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{2}
,

\displaystyle\big\|u(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{q^{\prime}}=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big(\frac{\big|u\big|}{\sqrt{u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}}\big)^{q^{\prime}}(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))\leq\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})),

we may conclude by Lebesgue’s theorem on dominated convergence that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\Delta u\cdot u(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\Delta u\cdot u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}u\cdot u(u^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

and identity (
9
) follows.

Having identity (
9
) at hand, we may proceed as follows: Via the boundary trace map, the space
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2)}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})
embeds continuously into
\mathrm{L}_{q}((0,\tau);\mathrm{W}_{q}^{1}(\Sigma))\subseteq\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1/2,1)}(\Sigma_{\tau})
by comparing the parabolic Sobolev indices
2-\frac{d+2}{q}\geq 1-\frac{d+1}{q}
(which is true for all
q\geq 1
) and, therefore, as
q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{q-1}
and
\big|u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\big|^{q^{\prime}}=\big|u\big|^{q}
it follows that
u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})
for every
u\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
with
\big\|u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq C\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{q-1}
and
u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})
with
\big\|u\big|u\big|^{q-2}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{q-1}
.
Further,
\nabla u\cdot\nabla(u\big|u\big|^{q-2})=(q-1)\big|\nabla u\big|^{2}\big|u\big|^{q-2}\geq 0
.
Approximating
u\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
by functions in
\mathrm{C}^{2}(\overline{\Omega}_{\tau})
and employing Hölder’s inequality for the left-hand side in (
9
) and the boundary term on the right hand side, we may thus infer that the identity (
9
) is valid for all
u\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
, the integral
\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla(u\big|u\big|^{q-2})\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
exists for all
u\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
and can be bounded by
C\big\|u\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{q}
.

It follows that
\big|v\big|^{q/2}\in\mathrm{L}_{2}((0,T);\mathrm{W}^{1}_{2}(\Omega))
with
\big\|\big|v\big|^{q/2}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Omega_{\tau})}=\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{q/2}
and
\nabla\big|v\big|^{q/2}=\frac{q}{2}v\big|v\big|^{q/2-2}\nabla v
with
\mathrm{L}_{2}
-norm
\big\|\nabla\big|v\big|^{q/2}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Omega_{\tau})}=\frac{q}{2}\big\|\big|v\big|^{q/2-1}\nabla v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Omega_{\tau})}
.

Moreover, the
\Sigma
-trace of
\big|v\big|^{q/2}\in\mathrm{L}_{2}((0,T);\mathrm{W}_{2}^{1}(\Omega))
is
\big(\big|v\big|^{q/2}\big)|_{\Sigma}=\big|v|_{\Sigma}\big|^{q/2}\in\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Sigma_{\tau})
, so that

\displaystyle\big\|v|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{q}

\displaystyle=\big\|\big|v\big|^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{2}\leq C\big\|\big|v\big|^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}((0,\tau);\mathrm{H}^{s-1/2}(\Sigma))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\big|v\big|^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}((0,\tau);\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega))}^{2}\leq C\big\|\big|v\big|^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}((0,\tau);\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega))}^{2s}\big\|\big|v\big|^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}((0,\tau);\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Omega))}^{2(1-s)}

\displaystyle=C\big\|\big|v\big|^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}((0,\tau);\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega))}^{2s}\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{(1-s)q}.

∎

Adjusting the norm for which we want to estimate the surface trace
v|_{\Sigma}
in the previous result, we obtain the following variant using the duality method, cf.
[
22
]
.

Lemma 8.5

(Dual estimate (Variant))
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty]
,
T>0
and
\alpha,\beta>0
be fixed, and let
\tau\in(0,T]
.
Assume that
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{U}_{\tilde{p}}^{\Omega}(T)
with
\tilde{p}>d+2
.
Let
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})
,
g^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{\tau})
and
v_{0}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)
be given and assume that
-\beta\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v_{0}|_{\Sigma}=g^{\Sigma}|_{t=0}
if
p>3
.
Then there is a constant
C=C(q,T)>0
such that for every strong solution
v\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
of the initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle\alpha\partial_{t}v+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v-\dv(d\nabla v)

\displaystyle=f\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Omega_{\tau},

(10)

\displaystyle-d\partial_{\nu}v

\displaystyle=g^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},

\displaystyle v(0)

\displaystyle=v_{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

the following estimate is valid:

\displaystyle\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|v|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\left(\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|g^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}\right),

where we write

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\{g\in\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau}):\,g|_{t=\tau}=0\},&q<\frac{3}{2},\\
\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau}),&q\geq\frac{3}{2},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\{h\in\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau}):\,h|_{t=\tau}=0\},&q<3,\\
\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau}),&q\geq 3.\end{cases}

Proof.

In the spirit of the duality technique, let arbitrary
\Theta\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})
and
\eta\in\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})
such that (if
q<\frac{3}{2}
)
\eta|_{t=\tau}=0
be given and let
\theta\in\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
be the unique solution to the (time-reversed) dual problem

\begin{cases}-\alpha\partial_{t}\theta-\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\theta-\dv(d\nabla\theta)=\Theta\quad&\text{in }\Omega_{\tau},\\
-d\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\theta=\eta\quad&\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},\\
\theta(\tau,\cdot)=0\quad&\text{in }\Omega.\end{cases}

Thanks to
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}
-maximal regularity, this solution
\theta\in\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
exists and is uniquely determined.
Moreover, thanks to the condition
\theta(\tau,\cdot)=0
there is a constant
C=C(q,T)
, which is independent of
\tau\in(0,T]
such that

\big\|\theta\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|\Theta\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\eta\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big).

Using integration by parts and Green’s formula, we may conclude that (recall that
\theta(\tau,\cdot)=0
in
\Omega
,
\dv\boldsymbol{v}=0
in
\Omega
and
\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v\equiv 0
on
\Sigma_{\tau}
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}v\Theta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}v\eta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))
