\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}v(\alpha\partial_{t}\theta+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\theta+\dv(d\nabla\theta))\,\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}vd\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\theta\,\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}(\alpha\partial_{t}v+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v-\dv(d\nabla v))\theta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\theta d\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))-\int_{\Omega}v^{0}\theta(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}f\theta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g^{\Sigma}\theta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))-\int_{\Omega}v^{0}\theta(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

The right-hand side can be estimated by

\displaystyle\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big\|\theta\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|g^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big\|\theta\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\|v^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}\big\|\theta(0,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|g^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\big(\big\|\Theta\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\eta\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big).

Taking the supremum over all such
\Theta
and
\eta
with norm less or equal
1
, and using that
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})
is isometrically isomorphic to
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}
, we find that

\displaystyle\big\|v\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|v|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\big\|f\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|g^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}\big).

∎

Similar estimates are also valid for reaction-diffusion-advection systems on the surface
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
:

Lemma 8.6

(Dual estimates on the surface)
.

Let
\Sigma
be the boundary of a bounded
\mathrm{C}^{3-}
-domain
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
,
p,q\in(1,\infty]
,
T>0
and
\alpha,\beta>0
be fixed, and let
\tau\in(0,T]
.
Let
f^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})
and
v^{\Sigma,0}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Sigma)
.
Then there is a constant
C=C(q,T)>0
such that for every strong solution
v^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})
of the initial value problem

(11)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(d^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=f^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},

\displaystyle v^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=v^{\Sigma,0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma

the following estimate is valid:

\big\|v^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq C\left(\big\|f^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v^{\Sigma,0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Sigma)^{\ast}}\right).

Proof.

For arbitrary
\Theta^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})
, let us consider the dual, time-reversed problem

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}\Psi^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(d^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma})=\Theta^{\Sigma}\quad&\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},\\
\Psi^{\Sigma}(\tau,\cdot)=0\quad&\text{on }\Sigma,\end{cases}

which admits
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}
-maximal regularity, hence, has a unique solution
\Psi^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})
such that

\big\|\Psi^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq C\big\|\Theta^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

for some constant
C>0
, which is not only independent of
\Theta^{\Sigma}
, but of
\tau\in(0,T]
as well since we only consider zero final data
\Psi^{\Sigma}(\tau,\cdot)=0
.
We then calculate that

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}v^{\Sigma}\cdot\Theta^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}v^{\Sigma}(\partial_{t}\Psi^{\Sigma}+\dv_{\Sigma}(d^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma}))\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\partial_{t}v^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(d^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma}))\Psi^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma,0}\Psi^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}f^{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma,0}\Psi^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}),

which we can estimate by

\displaystyle\big\|f^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big\|\Psi^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|v^{\Sigma,0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Sigma)^{\ast}}\big\|\Psi^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\big\|f^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v^{\Sigma,0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Sigma)^{\ast}}\big)\big\|\Psi^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Sigma)^{\ast}},

and the assertion follows by taking the supremum over all
\Theta^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})
with
\big\|\Theta^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq 1
.
∎

We will also need a version of the duality estimate related to the positive part.
Such positive versions are well-established for the pure bulk phase case, where we once again refer to M. Pierre’s survey
[
52
]
.

Lemma 8.7

(Positive duality estimate)
.

Let
T>0
,
q\in(1,\infty]
and
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
be a bounded domain with
\mathrm{C}^{3-}
-boundary
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
.
Let
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{U}_{\tilde{p}}^{\Omega}(T)
for some
\tilde{p}>d+2
.
Given
f,f_{i}\in\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})
,
g^{\Sigma},g_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p})}(\Sigma_{\tau})
and
v^{0},v^{0}_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega)
(with compatibility condition
\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v^{0}=g^{\Sigma}|_{t=0}
and
\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v^{0}_{i}=g_{i}^{\Sigma}|_{t=0}
if
q>3
), let
v,v_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
be the (unique) strong
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}
-solutions to the parabolic initial-boundary value problems

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v-\dv(d\nabla v)

\displaystyle=f\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

\displaystyle d\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v

\displaystyle=g^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle v(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=v^{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

and

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\partial_{t}v_{i}+\beta_{i}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v_{i}-\dv(d_{i}\nabla v_{i})

\displaystyle=f_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

\displaystyle d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v_{i}

\displaystyle=g_{i}^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle v_{i}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=v^{0}_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

where
\tau\in(0,T)
, and positive constants
d,\alpha_{i},d_{i}>0
and
\beta_{i}\in\R
are given.

Then there is a constant
C=C(q,\Omega,T)>0
such that

\displaystyle\big\|v^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|v^{+}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|v_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|v_{j}^{+}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|(v^{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_{j}v^{0}_{j})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\|(f-\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|(g^{\Sigma}-\sum_{j=1}^{N}g_{j}^{\Sigma})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big).

Proof.

We proceed similar to the proof of the dual estimates, however, this time restrict ourselves to functions
\Theta\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})
and
\eta\in\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})
which are
non-negative
.
As in the proof of Lemma
8.5
, we consider the dual problem

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}\Psi-\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\Psi-\dv(d\nabla\Psi)=\Theta\quad&\text{in }\Omega_{\tau},\\
d\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\Psi=\eta\quad&\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},\\
\Psi(\tau,\cdot)=0\quad&\text{in }\Omega.\end{cases}

By
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}
-maximal regularity of this problem and the comparison principle for parabolic equations with inhomogeneous Neumann data, we obtain a unique and non-negative solution
\Psi\in\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
such that

\big\|\Psi\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|\Theta\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\eta\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of the time-horizon
\tau\in(0,T]
(thanks to the homogeneous final condition
\Psi(\tau,\cdot)=0
) and the data
(\Theta,\eta)
.
Then, using the PDE solved by
\theta
and
v
, and via integration by parts, we find that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}v\Theta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}v\eta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}v(\partial_{t}\Psi+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\Psi+\dv(d\nabla\Psi))\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}v\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}(\partial_{t}v+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v-\dv(d\nabla v))\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\Psi d\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Omega}v^{0}\Psi(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}f\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g^{\Sigma}\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Omega}v^{0}\Psi(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

The latter terms can be written as

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}f\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g^{\Sigma}\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Omega}v^{0}\Psi(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}(f-\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j})\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(g^{\Sigma}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{j}^{\Sigma})\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Omega}(v^{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{0})\Psi(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big(\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}f_{j}\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g_{j}^{\Sigma}\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Omega}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{0}\Psi(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\big).

Using the PDEs for the functions
v_{i}
, we further obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}f_{j}\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g_{j}^{\Sigma}\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Omega}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{0}\Psi(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}(\alpha_{j}\partial_{t}v_{j}+\beta_{j}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v_{j}-\dv(d_{j}\nabla v_{j})\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\Psi d_{j}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v_{j}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Omega}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{0}\Psi(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}v_{j}\big(\alpha_{j}\partial_{t}\Psi+\beta_{j}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\Psi+\dv(d_{j}\nabla\Psi)\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\frac{d_{j}}{d}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\eta v_{j}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})).

Together, this gives

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}v\Theta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}v\eta\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}(f-\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j})\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(g^{\Sigma}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{j}^{\Sigma})\Psi\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Omega}(v^{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{0})\Psi(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big(\int_{\Omega_{\tau}}v_{j}\big(\alpha_{j}\partial_{t}\Psi+\beta_{j}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\Psi+\dv(d_{j}\nabla\Psi)\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\frac{d_{j}}{d}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\eta v_{j}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))\big)

\displaystyle\leq\big\|(f-\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big\|\Psi\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\|(g^{\Sigma}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{j}^{\Sigma})^{+}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big\|\Psi|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\|(v^{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{0})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}\big\|\Psi(0,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big\|v_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big\|\alpha_{j}\partial_{t}\Psi+\beta_{j}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\Psi+\dv(d_{j}\nabla\Psi)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{d_{j}}{d}\big\|v_{j}^{+}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big\|\eta\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})},

since we know that
\Psi\geq 0
and
\eta\geq 0
.
Here, by
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}
-maximal regularity, we find that

\big\|\alpha_{j}\partial_{t}\Psi+\beta_{j}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\Psi+\dv(d_{j}\nabla\Psi)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|\Theta\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\eta\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big),

so that taking the supremum over all non-negative
\Theta
and
\eta
with norm less or equal
1
, we may conclude that

\displaystyle\big\|v^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|v^{+}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\big\|(f-\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|(g^{\Sigma}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{j}^{\Sigma})^{+}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\quad+\big\|(v^{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{0})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big\|v_{j}^{+}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big).

∎

Similarly, a positive version of the dual estimate for parabolic equations on the surface
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
can be derived:

Lemma 8.8

(Positive dual estimate on the surface)
.

Let
\Sigma\subseteq\R^{d}
be the boundary of a bounded
\mathrm{C}^{3-}
-domain,
\mu,\beta_{i}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\Sigma;(0,\infty))
and
\alpha_{i}\in\R
,
i=1,\ldots,m
.
Moreover, let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
and
T>0
be given.
Assume that
v^{\Sigma}
,
v_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})
are solutions to the parabolic initial-value problems

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(\mu\nabla_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma})=f^{\Sigma}\quad&\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},\\
v^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)=v^{\Sigma,0}\quad&\text{on }\Sigma\end{cases}

and

\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}\partial_{t}v_{i}^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(\beta_{i}\nabla_{\Sigma}v_{i}^{\Sigma})=f_{i}^{\Sigma}\quad&\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},\\
v_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)=v_{i}^{\Sigma,0}\quad&\text{on }\Sigma\end{cases}

for some
\tau\in(0,T]
and
f^{\Sigma},f_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})
,
v^{\Sigma,0}
,
v_{i}^{\Sigma,0}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Sigma)
.

Then there is a constant
C=C(q,T)
, independent of
\tau\in(0,T]
and the data, such that

\displaystyle\big\|v^{\Sigma,+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\big\|(f^{\Sigma}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}^{\Sigma})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|(v^{\Sigma,0}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{\Sigma,0})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Sigma)^{\ast}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big\|v_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big).

Proof.

Similar to the bulk case, we consider an arbitrary non-negative function
\Theta^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})
and the time-reversed dual problem

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}\Psi^{\Sigma}-\dv(\mu\nabla_{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma})=\Theta^{\Sigma}\quad&\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},\\
\Psi^{\Sigma}(\tau,\cdot)=0\quad&\text{on }\Sigma\end{cases}

which has the property of
\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}
-maximal regularity with a maximal regularity constant which, thanks to the zero final conditions, can be chosen independent of the time horizon
\tau\in(0,T]
, i.e.

\big\|\Psi^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq C\big\|\Theta^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau})}.

Moreover, as
\Theta^{\Sigma}\geq 0
, by the parabolic positivity principle,
\Psi^{\Sigma}\geq 0
, and, therefore, we may estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}v^{\Sigma}\Theta^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\partial_{t}\Psi^{\Sigma}+\dv_{\Sigma}(\mu\nabla_{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma}))v^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\partial_{t}v^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(\mu\nabla_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma}))\Psi^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma,0}\Psi^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}f^{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Sigma}v^{\Sigma,0}\Psi^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(f^{\Sigma}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}^{\Sigma})\Psi^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Sigma}(v^{\Sigma,0}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{\Sigma,0})\Psi^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big(\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}f_{j}^{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Sigma}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{\Sigma,0}\Psi^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})\big).

Here, we may estimate the terms in the last line by

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}f_{j}^{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Sigma}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{\Sigma,0}\Psi^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big(\alpha_{j}\partial_{t}v_{j}^{\Sigma}\cdot\Psi^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(\beta_{j}\nabla_{\Sigma}v_{j}^{\Sigma})\Psi^{\Sigma}\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))+\int_{\Sigma}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big(\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{\Sigma}\partial_{t}\Psi^{\Sigma}+v_{j}^{\Sigma}\dv_{\Sigma}(\beta_{j}\nabla_{\Sigma}\Psi^{\Sigma})\big)\,\mathrm{d}(t,\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}))

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|v_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big\|\Theta^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})}.

Taking the supremum over all non-negative
\Theta^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{q^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})
with norm less or equal one, we deduce that

\displaystyle\big\|v^{\Sigma,+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\big\|(f^{\Sigma}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}^{\Sigma})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|(v^{\Sigma,0}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{\Sigma,0})^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Sigma)^{\ast}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big\|v_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big).

∎

9.
Global-in-time existence

By comparing with blow-up results for ODEs modelling reaction networks, it is clear that for homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions global-in-time well-posedness of reaction-diffusion systems cannot be expected, in general, and blow-up may occur (but see
[
69
]
for a quite general result under mass dissipation and an entropy type dissipation condition).
Therefore, we cannot expect global-in-time existence of solutions without any further restriction on the structure of the chemical reaction networks in the bulk phase and on the surface.
Also note the blow-up result in
[
53
]
for
inhomogeneous
Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Example 9.1

(Blow-up in the Henry model)
.

Consider
N=1
and the Henry model
s^{\Sigma}(c|_{\Sigma},c^{\Sigma})=c-c^{\Sigma}
, chemical reaction models
r^{\Omega}(c)=r^{\Sigma}(c)=c^{2}
, and initial data
c^{0}\equiv 1
,
c^{\Sigma,0}\equiv 1
.
(Here, for simplicity, we set all physical parameters to be
1
.)
Then the unique solution
(c,c^{\Sigma})(t,\cdot)\equiv(\frac{1}{1-t},\frac{1}{1-t})
,
t\in[0,1)
, blows up in finite time.

Although this example is of purely academic nature, it hints that also for systems with coupling between bulk and surface reaction-diffusion-advection systems, further structural assumptions are needed to ensure global-in-time existence of solutions.
Throughout this section we, therefore, additionally demand the following of the chemical reaction models
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}
in the bulk phase and
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
on the active surface, respectively.
Note that for the case of Maxwell–Stefan diffusion models, where the diffusive flux is modelled by a indirect balance equation of forces under a summation constraint,
\mathrm{L}_{\infty}
-bounds are trivial, but do, in general, not suffice to establish global-in-time existence of solutions, cf.
[
21
]
.

Assumption 9.2

(Triangular structure of
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}
and
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}
/ Intermediate sum condition)
.

There are lower triangular matrices
\boldsymbol{Q}^{\Omega},\boldsymbol{Q}^{\Sigma}\in\R_{+}^{N\times N}
with strictly positive diagonal entries and
C_{\mathrm{tr}}
,
C_{\mathrm{tr}}^{\Sigma}>0
such that

(
A_{S}^{\mathrm{bulk}}
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}^{\Omega}\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{tr}}\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}y_{j}\right)^{\mu^{\Omega}}\boldsymbol{e}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for all }\boldsymbol{y}\in[0,\infty)^{N},

(
A_{S}^{\mathrm{ch}}
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}^{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{tr}}^{\Sigma}\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}y^{\Sigma}_{j}\right)^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\boldsymbol{e}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for all }\boldsymbol{y}^{\Sigma}\in[0,\infty)^{N},

for some exponents
\mu^{\Omega},\mu^{\Sigma}\geq 0
, and where
\boldsymbol{e}=(1,\ldots,1)^{\mathsf{T}}\in\R^{N}
and we write
\boldsymbol{v}\leq\boldsymbol{w}
if
v_{i}\leq w_{i}
for all components
i=1,\ldots,N
.

To make a boot-strap argument feasible, and eventually establish
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-growth bounds for the solution, we need
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates as in the following lemma, which heavily exploits the triangular structure of the chemical reaction networks.

Lemma 9.3

(
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates)
.

Assume that, additionally to the standing Assumption
5.1
, also the intermediate sum condition Assumption
9.2
is satisfied.
Then, for

p\in(\max\{1,\frac{(\mu^{\Omega}-1)(d+2)}{2\mu^{\Omega}},\frac{(\mu^{\Sigma}-1)(d+1)}{2\mu^{\Sigma}}\},\infty),\quad q\in(1,\infty]\quad\text{and}\quad T_{0}>0,

we set
T^{\ast}:=\min\{T_{0},T_{\mathrm{max}}\}\in(0,\infty)
, and there is a constant
C^{\ast}=C^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0},p,q,T^{\ast})
such that for all
\tau\in(0,T^{\ast}]

\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau};\R^{N})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathbb{G}}_{q^{\prime}}^{\Sigma}(\tau)^{\ast}}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq C^{\ast}\left(1+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau};\R^{N})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau};\R^{N})}\right)^{\mu},

where
\mu=\mu(\mu^{\Omega},\mu^{\Sigma},p,q)
will, in general, depend on
\mu^{\Omega}
,
\mu^{\Sigma}
as well as on
p
and
q
.

Remark 9.4
.

In case
\mu^{\Omega}=\mu^{\Sigma}=1
, i.e. a
linear
intermediate sum condition, all
p,q\in(1,\infty)
are admissible for the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates in this lemma.

Proof of Lemma
9.3
.

First, we consider the case where
q>p
are such that
2-\frac{d+2}{p}>-\frac{d+1}{q}
,
2-\frac{d+2}{p}>-\frac{d+2}{\mu^{\Omega}q}
and
2-\frac{d+1}{p}>-\frac{d+1}{\mu^{\Sigma}q}
, so that the continuous embeddings
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{T})
(via the boundary trace map),
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{\mu^{\Omega}q}(\Omega_{\tau})
and
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{\mu^{\Sigma}q}(\Sigma_{\tau})
are available.
Here, the assumption that
p>\max\{\frac{(\mu^{\Omega}-1)(d+2)}{2\mu^{\Omega}},\frac{(\mu^{\Sigma}-1)(d+1)}{2\mu^{\Sigma}}\}
enables us to find
q>p
such that the latter two conditions are met.

We employ the triangular structure of the chemical reaction network in the bulk phase and on the active surface, by which there are constants
C_{\mathrm{tr}},C_{\mathrm{tr}}^{\Sigma}>0
such that for
i=1,\ldots,N
and
r_{ij}:=\frac{q_{ij}}{q_{ii}},r_{ij}^{\Sigma}=\frac{q_{ij}^{\Sigma}}{q_{ii}^{\Sigma}}\in\R
, the inequalities

\displaystyle r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{tr}}(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}c_{j})^{\mu^{\Omega}}-\sum_{j<i}r_{ij}r^{\Omega}_{j}(\boldsymbol{c})

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{tr}}(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}c_{j})^{\mu^{\Omega}}-\sum_{j<i}r_{ij}(\partial_{t}c_{j}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{j}-d_{j}\Delta c_{j})

\displaystyle r^{\Sigma}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{tr}}^{\Sigma}(1+\sum_{j=1}c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}-\sum_{j<i}r_{ij}^{\Sigma}r^{\Sigma}_{j}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{tr}}^{\Sigma}(1+\sum_{j=1}c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}-\sum_{j<i}r_{ij}^{\Sigma}(\partial_{t}c^{\Sigma}_{j}-d_{j}^{\Sigma}\Delta_{\Sigma}c^{\Sigma}_{j}-s_{j}(c_{j}|_{\Sigma},c_{j}^{\Sigma}))

are valid.

We use these estimates in combination with the positive version of the duality estimates stated in Lemma
8.7
and Lemma
8.8
:
Fix any
T_{0}>0
, set
T^{\ast}:=\min\{T_{0},T_{\mathrm{max}}\}
and let
\tau\in(0,T^{\ast})
, where
T_{\mathrm{max}}\in(0,\infty]
is the maximal time of existence for the solution to the reaction-diffusion-advection-sorption system (
RDASS
) in the class
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
for some
p\in(1,\infty)
(and then, as it will turn out, away from
t=0
for
every

p\in(1,\infty)
, see Lemma
9.5
below).
For each component
i=1,\ldots,N
of the system, we consider the following auxiliary parabolic initial-boundary value problems:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla z_{i}-d_{i}\Delta z_{i}

\displaystyle=r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

(a)

\displaystyle-d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}z_{i}

\displaystyle=-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}(1+c_{i}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle z_{i}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=c^{0}_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Omega

in the bulk phase and the parabolic initial value problems

(b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z_{i}^{\Sigma}-d_{i}\Delta_{\Sigma}z_{i}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=r^{\Sigma}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})+k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle z_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=c^{\Sigma,0}_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma

on the boundary.
Choosing
r\in(1,\min\{p,3\})
arbitrarily, the initial data do not necessarily have to satisfy the compatibility conditions, so that we obtain unique solutions
z_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})
and
z_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})
of (
a
) and (
b
), respectively.
Moreover, since the lower and upper linear bounds
-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}(1+c_{i}^{\Sigma})\leq r^{\mathrm{sorp}}_{i}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})\leq k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1+c_{i})
are valid by assumption (

A
B

sorp

), the comparison principle Lemma
8.1
provides the estimates

0\leq c_{i}\leq z_{i},\quad 0\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma}\leq z_{i}^{\Sigma}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots N.

We exploit that the systems (
a
) and (
b
) are quasi-linear and write
z_{i}=u_{i}+v_{i}+w_{i}
and
z_{i}^{\Sigma}=u_{i}^{\Sigma}+v_{i}^{\Sigma}+w_{i}^{\Sigma}
as a sum of solutions to the following quasi-autonomous, linear systems:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla u_{i}-d_{i}\Delta u_{i}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{tr}}(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}c_{j})^{\mu^{\Omega}}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

(a1)

\displaystyle-d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}u_{i}

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle u_{i}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla v_{i}-d_{i}\Delta v_{i}

\displaystyle=r_{i}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})-C_{\mathrm{tr}}(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}c_{j})^{\mu^{\Omega}}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

(a2)

\displaystyle-d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v_{i}

\displaystyle=-k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}(1+c_{i}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle v_{i}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla w_{i}-d_{i}\Delta w_{i}

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,\tau)\times\Omega,

(a3)

\displaystyle-d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}w_{i}

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle w_{i}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=c^{0}_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

in the bulk phase, whereas on the surface, we consider the problems

(b1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{i}^{\Sigma}-d_{i}^{\Sigma}\Delta_{\Sigma}u_{i}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{tr}}^{\Sigma}(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}+k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1+c_{i}|_{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle u_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

and

(b2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v_{i}^{\Sigma}-d_{i}^{\Sigma}\Delta_{\Sigma}v_{i}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=r^{\Sigma}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})-C_{\mathrm{tr}}^{\Sigma}(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle v_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

and

(b3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w_{i}^{\Sigma}-d_{i}^{\Sigma}\Delta_{\Sigma}w_{i}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle w_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=c^{\Sigma,0}_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\tau)\times\Sigma.

Since the strong solutions
c_{j},c_{j}^{\Sigma}\geq 0
are non-negative, by the comparison principle, we immediately find that the solutions
u_{i},w_{i},u_{i}^{\Sigma},w_{i}^{\Sigma}
to the initial (boundary) value problems (
a1
), (
a3
), (
b1
) and (
b3
) are non-negative, whereas for the solutions
v_{i}
and
v_{i}^{\Sigma}
to (
a2
) and (
b2
) non-negativity cannot be guaranteed. However, we always have the inequalities

0\leq c_{i}\leq z_{i}\leq u_{i}+v_{i}^{+}+w_{i},\quad 0\leq c_{i}^{\Sigma}\leq z_{i}^{\Sigma}\leq u_{i}^{\Sigma}+v_{i}^{\Sigma,+}+w_{i}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,N.

Let us collect the information which we can extract from the duality estimates and positive duality estimates:

\big\|u_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|u_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\leq C_{p,q,T^{\ast}}(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|c_{j}^{\mu^{\Omega}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}),

where the constant
C_{p,q,T^{\ast}}>0
may be chosen independent of
\tau<T^{\ast}
, since we consider zero initial data in (
a1
);

\displaystyle\big\|v_{i}^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|v_{i}^{+}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{p,q,T}(1+\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|c_{j}^{\mu^{\Omega}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}),

\displaystyle\big\|w_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|w_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\leq C_{q,T^{\ast}}.

Here, in the latter two estimates the constants
C_{p,q,T^{\ast}}
and
C_{q,T^{\ast}}
may (and will) depend on the initial data, but not on the particular choice of
\tau\in(0,T^{\ast})
.
Similarly, on the surface we obtain the estimates

\displaystyle\big\|u_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q,T}(1+\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}})

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q,T}(1+\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}})

again exploiting the zero initial data;

\displaystyle\big\|v_{i}^{\Sigma,+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q,T^{\ast}}\sum_{j<i}\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})};

\displaystyle\big\|w_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q,T^{\ast}}.

We now bring together these estimates, proceeding iteratively:

i=1:

For the surface term, we find

\displaystyle\big\|c_{1}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|z_{1}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq\big\|u_{1}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|v_{1}^{\Sigma,+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|w_{1}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q,T^{\ast}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|c_{1}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big)

and, for the bulk term,

\displaystyle\big\|c_{1}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{1}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|z_{1}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|z_{1}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|u_{1}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|u_{1}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v_{1}^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\quad+\big\|v_{1}^{+}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|w_{1}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|w_{1}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q,T^{\ast}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|(c_{j})^{\mu^{\Omega}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|c_{1}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big).

Together, this gives the estimate

\displaystyle\big\|c_{1}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{1}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|c_{1}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{q,T^{\ast}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|c_{j}^{\mu^{\Omega}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})\ast}\big).

i\geq 2
:

Now, assume that
i\geq 2
and for
k=1,\ldots,i-1
we have shown the estimates

\displaystyle\big\|c_{k}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{k}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|c_{k}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{q,T^{\ast}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|c_{j}^{\mu^{\Omega}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big).

We then find

\displaystyle\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|z_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq\big\|u_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|v_{i}^{\Sigma,+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|w_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{q,T^{\ast}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\sum_{j<i}\big\|c_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big).

and

\displaystyle\big\|c_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|z_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|z_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|u_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|u_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v_{i}^{+}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\quad+\big\|v_{i}^{+}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|w_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|w_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{q,T^{\ast}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|c_{j}^{\mu^{\Omega}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\sum_{j<i}\big(\big\|c_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{j}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|c_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big)\big).

Together with the induction hypothesis, this gives

\displaystyle\big\|c_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{q,T^{\ast}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\|c_{j}^{\mu^{\Omega}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big),

so that by iteration we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(\big\|c_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{j}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|c_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big)

(12)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{q,T^{\ast}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(\big\|c_{j}^{\mu^{\Omega}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|(c_{j}^{\Sigma})^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big)\big).

From the estimate (
12
), we may now derive the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates.
First, let us consider the case that
p<q\leq\infty
(hence,
1\leq q^{\prime}<p^{\prime}
for the corresponding Hölder conjugates) such that
\frac{1}{q}\geq\frac{1}{p}-\frac{d+2}{2}
,
2-\frac{d+2}{p}\geq-\frac{d+2}{\mu^{\Sigma}q}
.
Then, we may employ the continuous and dense embeddings

\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{(\mu^{\Omega}p)^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\tau})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{(\mu^{\Sigma}p)^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\tau}),

and, hence, we may conclude that

\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}

are continuously embedded.
Therefore, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(\big\|c_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{j}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q,T^{*}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(\big\|c_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{\mu^{\Omega}}+\big\|c_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{\mu^{\Sigma}}\big)\big)\quad\text{for }p<q\leq\infty\text{ s.t.\ }\frac{1}{p}\geq\frac{1}{q}-\frac{d+2}{2}.

Iterating this procedure, if necessary, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(\big\|c_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{j}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q,T^{*}}\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(\big\|c_{j}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{j}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)\big)^{\mu}

for all
p>\max\{1,\frac{(\mu^{\Omega}-1)(d+2)}{2\mu^{\Omega}},\frac{(\mu^{\Sigma}-1)(d+1)}{2\mu^{\Sigma}}\},\,q\in(1,\infty)
,
where
\mu\geq 0
will, in general, be a multiple of
\mu^{\Omega}
or
\mu^{\Sigma}
.
∎

Regarding the term
c_{j}|_{\Sigma}
, this is not yet the estimate in the norm which we are actually looking for.
To improve on the norm, we consider the bulk reaction-diffusion-advection problems

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla-d_{i}\Delta)c_{i}

\displaystyle=f_{i}:=r_{i}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\tau},

\displaystyle c_{i}

\displaystyle=h_{i}:=c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},

\displaystyle c_{i}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=c_{i}^{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

For the initial data, we may and will w.l.o.g. assume that
c_{i}^{0}\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Moreover, by the estimates which we have already established and polynomial boundedness of the chemical reaction rates, we may conclude that
r_{i}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})\in\bigcap\limits_{q\in(1,\infty)}\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})
and
h_{i}=c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\in\bigcap\limits_{q\in(1,\infty)}\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}
.

To obtain an estimate of the form

\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{\gamma^{\Omega}}+\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big)\quad\text{for sufficiently large }q,

we need to make some additional considerations:
As a first step, let us consider the known optimal regularity estimate

\big\|v_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|h_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega)}+\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\big)

for the reaction-diffusion-advection systems with inhomogeneous Dirichlet data

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla-d_{i}\Delta)v_{i}

\displaystyle=f_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\tau},

\displaystyle v_{i}|_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=h_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},

\displaystyle v_{i}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=v_{i}^{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

Via its dual problem

\displaystyle-(\partial_{t}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla+d_{i}\Delta)\theta_{i}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\tau},

\displaystyle\theta_{i}

\displaystyle=g_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},

\displaystyle\theta_{i}(\tau,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

we obtain the estimate

\big\|v_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|h_{i}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}+\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast}}\big).

We now want to interpolate between these estimates.
If we would proceed directly with the real interpolation method, we can estimate the
(\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau}),\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau}))_{\theta,q}
-norm (which is equivalent to
\big\|\cdot\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\theta}(\Omega_{\tau})}
) by the interpolation space norms

\displaystyle\big\|h_{i}\big\|_{(\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast},\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q})})_{\theta,q}},\quad\big\|v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{(\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast},\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega))_{\theta,q}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{and}\quad\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{(\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})^{\ast},\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau}))_{\theta,q}}

of
h_{i}
,
v_{i}^{0}
and
f_{i}
, respectively.
This direct approach, however, runs into the problem of how to characterize these interpolation spaces, e.g. by identifying them with suitable Besov or, more general, Triebel–Lizorkin spaces, if possible.
Unfortunately, the test functions
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)=\mathrm{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
are
not dense
in
\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)
for sufficiently large
q^{\prime}>1
(i.e. for small
q>1
), and, therefore, the characterizations which are valid for the full space
\R^{d}
do, in general, not remain true if one replaces
\R^{d}
by an open, regular domain
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
.
In fact, in this case the dual spaces also include elements which are
no distributions
.
We refer to
[
7
]
for results in the context of
corners
, i.e. intersections of open or closed half-spaces, in
\R^{d}
.
In our situation, however, we want to estimate an element in the dual space for which will
a-priori
know that it is represented by an – at least locally – integrable function; in particular a distribution.
To avoid the problems sketched above, we use that thanks to the regularity of the domain there exist
universal extension operators

\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}:\mathrm{L}_{1}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{1}(\R^{d})
and
\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}:\mathrm{L}_{1}(\Omega_{\tau})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{1}(\R^{1+d})
, cf.
[
62
]
, such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}|_{\mathrm{W}_{r}^{s}(\Omega)}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{r}^{s}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathrm{W}_{r}^{s}(\R^{d}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{d}}|_{\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\Omega_{\tau})}:

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\Omega_{\tau})\rightarrow\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\R^{1+d}),\quad r\in(1,\infty),\,s\in[0,2],

i.e. for each
r\in(1,\infty)
,
s\in[0,2]
, these are
bounded linear operators
, and the action of
\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}
resp.
\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}
does not depend on
r
and
s
, e.g.
\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}^{(r,s)}u=\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}^{(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}u
, whenever
u\in\mathrm{W}_{r}^{s}(\Omega)\cap\mathrm{W}_{r^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}
for some
r,r^{\prime}\in(1,\infty)
and
s,s^{\prime}\in[0,2]
.
Thanks to the regularity of the domain
\Omega
, these exist.
Moreover, for the Dirichlet trace function, we consider a smooth decomposition
\{\psi_{k}:\,k=1,\ldots,m\}
of unity in a neighbourhood subordinate to an open covering
\{U_{k}:k=1,\ldots,m\}
of the compact set
\overline{\Omega}
, and
\mathrm{C}^{2}
-isomorphisms
\Phi_{k}:U_{k}\rightarrow V_{k}\subseteq\R^{d}
such that
\Phi_{k}(U_{k}\cap\Omega)=V_{k}\cap(\R^{d-1}\times(0,\infty))
and
\Phi_{k}(U_{k}\cap\Sigma)=V_{k}\cap(\R^{d-1}\times\{0\})
, and the map
\mathcal{T}
defined by

(\mathcal{T}u)_{k}(t,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}(\psi_{k}u)(t,\Phi_{k}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},0)),&\text{if }(x^{\prime},0)\in V_{k},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Note that
\mathcal{T}|_{\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\Sigma_{\tau})}
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\Sigma_{\tau})
to
[\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\R\times\R^{d-1})]^{m}
for each
r\in(1,\infty)
and
s\in[0,2]
.
In fact, it is a topologic linear isomorphism between
\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\Sigma_{\tau})
and
\mathcal{T}(\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\Sigma_{\tau}))\subseteq\mathrm{W}_{r}^{(1,2)\cdot s}(\R\times\R^{d-1})
for each
r\in(1,\infty)
and
s\in[0,2]
.
We then find that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}v_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{1+d})}\leq\big\|v_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|h_{i}\big\|_{\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})+\big\|v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{2-2/q^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{\ast}}+\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|\mathcal{T}h_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(-\frac{1}{2q})}(\R^{1+d})}+\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(-2/q)}(\R^{d})}+\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(-1)}(\R^{1+d})}\right)

and

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}v_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big\|v_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|h_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q})}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega)}+\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{\tau})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|\mathcal{T}h_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2q})}(\R^{1+(d-1)};\R^{N})}+\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\R^{d})}+\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{1+d})}\right).

We may now interpolate between these inequalities with the real interpolation functor
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\theta,q}
for some
\theta\in(0,1)
and obtain that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}v_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\theta}(\R^{1+d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|\mathcal{T}h_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\theta-\frac{1}{2q})}(\R^{1+d};\R^{N})}+\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{2(\theta-\frac{1}{q})}(\R^{d})}+\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\theta-1)}(\R^{1+d})}\right).

For the particular choice
\theta=\frac{1}{2q}
, we find that the spatial trace space of
\mathrm{B}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\frac{1}{2q}}(\R^{1+d})
for the restriction to
\R\times\Sigma
continuously embeds into
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R\times\Sigma)
, so that we may conclude that

\displaystyle\big\|(\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}v_{i})|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R\times\Sigma)}\leq C\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega_{\tau}\rightarrow\R^{1+d}}v_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\theta}(\R^{1+d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|\mathcal{T}h_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\theta-\frac{1}{2q})}(\R^{1+(d-1)};\R^{N})}+\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{2(\theta-\frac{1}{q})}(\R^{d})}+\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{B}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\theta-1)}(\R^{1+d})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\big\|\mathcal{T}h_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{1+(d-1)};\R^{N})}+\big\|\mathcal{E}_{\Omega\rightarrow\R^{d}}v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\R^{d})}+\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{1+d})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q^{\prime}}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}})}(\Sigma_{\tau})^{\ast}}+\big\|v_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega)}+\big\|c_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{\gamma^{\Omega}}\right).

Lemma 9.5

(Parabolic smoothing)
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T})^{N}\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T})^{N}
be the solution to the reaction-diffusion-advection-sorption system (
RDASS
) with Langmuir adsorption for some
p\in(\max\{1,\frac{\mu^{\Omega}-1}{\mu^{\Omega}}\frac{d+2}{2},\frac{\mu^{\Sigma}-1}{\mu^{\Sigma}}\frac{d+1}{2}\},\infty)
and some
T>0
.
Then,
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega)^{N}\times\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma))^{N}
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,T)
and
q\in(1,\infty)
.

Remark 9.6
.

One might suspect that the smoothing property of the reaction-diffusion-advection-sorption system under consideration is valid
independently
of the particular triangular structure imposed on the reactive terms.
Note, however, that we also include the case of rather low values of
p\in(1,\infty)
.
In contrast to the case
p>\frac{d+2}{2}
, the solution space
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})
does, in general, not continuously embed into those spaces which would be required to employ Hölder regularity theory of reaction-diffusion equations.
In fact, if
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C}^{(1,2)\cdot\alpha}(\overline{\Omega_{\tau}})
,
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C}^{(1,2)\cdot(\alpha+\frac{1}{2})}(\overline{\Sigma_{\tau}})
(via the boundary trace map), and
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{\tau})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C}^{(1,2)\cdot\alpha}(\overline{\Sigma_{\tau}})
embed continuously for some
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
(which is always true if
p>\frac{d+2}{2}
), we could exploit the theory of quasi-autonomous, linear parabolic equations to deduce that actually
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{C}^{(1,2)\cdot(1+\alpha)}((\varepsilon,\tau);\R^{N})\times\mathrm{C}^{(1,2)\cdot(1+\alpha)}((\varepsilon,\tau)\times\Sigma;\R^{N})
for every
\varepsilon>0
.

In
[
64
]
, the smoothing property has been demonstrated for the case
p>d
.
In fact, from their results if follows that if
p>d
and
(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Sigma;\R^{N})
(satisfying the compatibility conditions), there is a unique classical solution in the class

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{C}([0,\tau];\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))\cap\mathrm{C}^{1}((0,\tau];\mathrm{C}(\overline{\Omega};\R^{N})\times\mathrm{C}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))

\displaystyle\qquad\cap\mathrm{C}((0,\tau];\mathrm{C}^{2}(\overline{\Omega};\R^{N})\times\mathrm{C}^{2}(\Sigma;\R^{N})).

Note that the condition
p>d
is sufficient to imply that
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C}^{1+\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
for
\alpha:=1-\frac{d}{p}\in(0,1)
, and then
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Sigma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C}^{1+\alpha}(\Sigma)
as well, so that the tools from Hölder regularity theory for parabolic equations become applicable.

Proof of Lemma
9.5
.

By the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates of Lemma
9.3
(which crucially depend on the triangular structure!), we know that
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})
and
\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma}
,
\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
By the polynomial boundedness of the chemical reaction terms
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})
and
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
as well as the sorption model
\boldsymbol{r}^{\mathrm{sorp}}(\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
, we may, thus, conclude that these are functions in
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{T})
and
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{T})
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
as well.
Therefore, the solutions
c_{i}^{\Sigma}:[0,T]\rightarrow\R
of the problems

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\Sigma}-d_{i}^{\Sigma}\Delta_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=F_{i}^{\Sigma}+G_{i}^{\Sigma}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

\displaystyle c_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=c_{i}^{\Sigma,0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma,

where
F_{i}^{\Sigma}=r_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
and
G_{i}^{\Sigma}=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{L}_{q}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma)
lie in
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma)
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T)
.
In fact, we may employ the representation

c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)=\mathrm{e}^{tA_{i}^{\Sigma}}c_{i}^{\Sigma,0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{(t-s)A_{i}^{\Sigma}}(F_{i}^{\Sigma}+G_{i}^{\Sigma})(s,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}s,

where the inhomogeneous term is in
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T})
since
F_{i}^{\Sigma}+G_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{T})
and by
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-maximal regularity, for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
For the homogeneous part
\mathrm{e}^{tA_{i}^{\Sigma}}c_{i}^{\Sigma,0}
we may use the smoothing effect of the analytic
C_{0}
-semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{tA_{i}^{\Sigma}})
on the space
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)
, so that this term lies in
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma)
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,T)
.
In particular,
c_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{C}((0,T];\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Sigma))
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
Concerning the bulk term, we see that
c_{i}|_{t\in[\varepsilon,T]}
is the solution to the linear parabolic initial-boundary value problem

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-d_{i}\Delta c_{i}=F_{i}&\text{in }(\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega,\\
k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1-\frac{c_{i}^{\Sigma,+}}{c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty}})^{+}+d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}c_{i}=G_{i}&\text{on }(\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma,\\
c_{i}(\varepsilon,0)=c_{i}^{\varepsilon}&\text{in }\overline{\Omega}\end{cases}

for
c_{i}^{\varepsilon}:=c_{i}(\varepsilon,0)\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)
, and
F_{i}=r_{i}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})\in\mathrm{L}_{q}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega)
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
,
G_{i}=k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}c_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma)
for all
q\in(q,\infty)
.
Now, we may write
c_{i}|_{t\in[\varepsilon,T]}=u_{i}+v_{i}+w_{i}
, where
u_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega
) for all
q\in(1,\infty)
with
\frac{1}{q}\geq\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{d+2}
is the solution to the parabolic initial-boundary value problem

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{i}-d_{i}\Delta u_{i}=F_{i}-\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}&\text{in }(\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega,\\
k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1-\frac{c_{i}^{\Sigma,+}}{c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty}})^{+}u_{i}+d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}u_{i}=G_{i}&\text{on }(\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma,\\
u_{i}(\varepsilon,\cdot)=0&\text{on }\overline{\Omega},\end{cases}

and where we may use the fact that
c_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{q}((\varepsilon,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma))
for
q\in(1,\infty)
such that
\frac{1}{q}\geq\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{d+2}
.
Moreover, the functions
v_{i}
and
w_{i}
are constructed by taking some
w_{i}^{\varepsilon}\in\bigcap_{q\in(1,\infty)}\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega)
such that
-d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}w_{i}=-k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1-\frac{c_{i}^{\Sigma,+}(\varepsilon,\cdot)}{c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty}})^{+}(c_{i}(\varepsilon,0)+w_{i}^{\varepsilon})
and then defining
v_{i}
and
w_{i}
as the solutions to the initial-boundary value problems

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{i}-d_{i}\Delta v_{i}=0&\text{in }(\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega,\\
d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}v_{i}=0&\text{on }(\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma,\\
v_{i}(\varepsilon,\cdot)=c_{i}(\varepsilon,0)-w_{i}^{\varepsilon}&\text{on }\overline{\Omega}\end{cases}

and

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}w_{i}-d_{i}\Delta w_{i}=0&\text{in }(\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega,\\
k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1-\frac{c_{i}^{\Sigma,+}}{c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty}})^{+}w_{i}+d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}w_{i}=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1-\frac{c_{i}^{\Sigma,+}}{c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty}})^{+}v_{i}&\text{on }(\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma,\\
w_{i}(\varepsilon,\cdot)=w_{i}^{\varepsilon}&\text{on }\Sigma.\end{cases}

Here, by
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-maximal regularity, the latter problem has a solution
w_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega)
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
Moreover, by classical smoothing properties of the Neumann semigroup on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)
, we obtain
v_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((2\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega)
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
Putting things together, this means that
c_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((2\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega)
for every
\varepsilon>0
, and, in particular,
c_{i}\in\mathrm{C}((0,T];\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega))
for every
q\in(1,\infty)
such that
\frac{1}{q}\geq\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{d+2}
.
Repeating the argument for
p^{\prime}\in(1,\infty)
such that
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}\geq\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{d+2}
and possibly multiple iterations, we obtain that actually
c_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega)
for all
\varepsilon>0
, and thus
c_{i}\in\mathrm{C}((0,T];\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega))
for
all

q\in(1,\infty)
.
∎

Corollary 9.7
.

If the strong
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}
-solution
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T};\R^{N})\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T};\R^{N})
exists for some
0<T<\infty
and
p\in(1,\infty)
, then its restriction to times
t\in(\varepsilon,T)
is a
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}
-solution
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}((\varepsilon,T)\times\Sigma;\R^{N})
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
. In particular, the maximal time of existence
T_{\mathrm{max}}\in(0,\infty]
does not depend on the choice of
p\in(1,\infty)
.
In particular, if

\sup_{t\in[\varepsilon,T]}\big\|(\boldsymbol{c}(t,\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot))\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}}<\infty

for some
p\in(1,\infty)
, then
\sup_{t\in[\varepsilon,T]}\big\|(\boldsymbol{c}(t,\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot))\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}}<\infty
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.

Once we have the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates at hand, we may easily infer exponential growth bounds for the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-norms of the solution on
\Omega_{\tau}
and
\Sigma_{\tau}
, respectively.

Lemma 9.8

(
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-growth bounds)
.

For every
T_{0}>0
,
p\in(1,\infty]
, there are
M=M(p,T_{0})>0
,
\omega=\omega(p,T_{0})\in\R
(also depending on the initial data) such that

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\big(\big\|c_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)\leq M\mathrm{e}^{\omega\tau},\quad\tau\in(0,\min\{T_{0},T_{\mathrm{max}}\}).

Proof.

We start by employing the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates of Lemma
9.3
for the special case
p\in(1,\infty)
and
q=\infty
, resulting in

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{c}(\tau,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)}^{p}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(\tau,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{c}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)}^{p}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)}^{p}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{p}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{p}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(1+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}^{p}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{p}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big[1+\int_{0}^{\tau}\big(\big\|\boldsymbol{c}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)}^{p}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)}^{p}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\big],\quad\tau\in(0,T_{0}]\cap(0,T_{\mathrm{max}}).

By Gronwall’s Lemma, see e.g.
[
9
, Lemma VII.1.15]
), there are, thus, constants
M^{\prime}=M^{\prime}_{p,T_{0}}>0
and
\omega^{\prime}=\omega^{\prime}_{p,T_{0}}>0
(also depending on the initial data through the constant
C
in the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates) such that

(13)

\big\|\boldsymbol{c}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)}^{p}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma)}^{p}\leq M^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{\omega^{\prime}t},\quad t\in(0,T_{0}]\times(0,T_{\mathrm{max}}).

Integrating over
t\in(0,\tau)
, this proves the claim for
p<\infty
.
The case
p=\infty
follows by integrating equation (
13
) in time for some finite
p\in(1,\infty)
and then employing the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{\infty}
-estimate provided by Lemma
9.3
.

Finally, we may proceed just as in the remarks after the proof of Lemma
9.3
to estimate the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-norm of
\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma}
as well.
∎

9.1.
Statement and proof of the global existence result

After these preparations, we are ready to prove the following theorem on global-in-time existence of solutions to the bulk-surface reaction-diffusion-advection system (
1
) for suitable initial data.

Theorem 9.9

(Global-in-time well-posedness)
.

Let
J=\R_{+}=[0,\infty)
,
p\in[\frac{d+2}{2},\infty)
and let Assumptions
5.1
and
9.2
be valid.
Moreover, assume that the sorption is modelled by the Langmuir sorption model.
Then, for all compatible initial data

(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma,0})\in\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Omega}\times\mathbb{I}_{p}^{\Sigma}

with
(\boldsymbol{c}^{0},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\geq(0,0)
, the reaction-diffusion-advection-sorption system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-\dv(d_{i}\nabla c_{i})

\displaystyle=r^{\Omega}_{i}(\boldsymbol{c})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\infty)\times\Omega,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\Sigma}-\dv_{\Sigma}(d_{i}^{\Sigma}\nabla_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})+r_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\infty)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle-d_{i}\partial_{\nu}c_{i}

\displaystyle=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,\infty)\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle c_{i}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=c^{0}_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

\displaystyle c_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=c^{\Sigma,0}_{i}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma,

for
i=1,\ldots,N
, has a unique global-in-time solution

(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{W}^{1}_{p,\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p,\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2}(\Sigma;\R^{N})).

Moreover, for every
q\in(1,\infty)
,

(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{W}^{1}_{q,\mathrm{loc}}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{q,\mathrm{loc}}((0,\infty);\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2}(\Sigma;\R^{N})).

The result is based on the comparison principle and the dual estimates for scalar reaction-diffusion-advection equations, i.e. a technique heavily used especially by the French school around Michel Pierre, cf. the survey
[
52
]
and related papers.
Note that we need regularity of class
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-2/p}
for some
p\geq\frac{d+2}{2}
to get a local-in time solution, which then immediately regularises, so that for any positive times
t>0
the solution lies in all spaces of class
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.

Proof.

We employ the following strategy to establish the global-in-time existence result, where thanks to the preparations done up to now, we will be able to go through each of these steps at fast pace:

(1)

We start with smooth initial data
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\bigcap_{q>1}(\mathbb{I}_{q}^{\Omega}\times\mathbb{I}_{q}^{\Sigma})
. More general initial data can be handled later on by exploiting the
smoothing effect
of the parabolic system, cf. Lemma
9.5
.

(2)

We fix an arbitrary time horizon
T_{0}>0
and consider
T^{\ast}=\min\{T_{0},T_{\mathrm{max}}\}
for the maximal time of existence
T_{\mathrm{max}}\in(0,\infty]
from the local-in-time existence result.
By the unique-continuation property, it suffices to show that the solution extends to
[0,T^{\ast}]
for all such
T^{\ast}>0
.

(3)

As a preliminary step, we demonstrate that the solution is in
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{T^{\ast}};\R^{N})\times\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{T^{\ast}};\R^{N})
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.

(4)

In combination with polynomial boundedness of the chemical reaction and surface ad- and desorption models, we may then derive
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-growth bounds for the reactive and sorption terms
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})
,
\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
and
\boldsymbol{s}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
.

(5)

By
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-maximal regularity of the linearised reaction-diffusion system on the surface, we can then show that the surface part actually lies in the class
\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{E}_{q}^{\Sigma}(T^{\ast})
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.

(6)

Only thereafter, we may exploit the
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-maximal regularity of the reaction-diffusion-advection system in the bulk phase with inhomogeneous Neumann boundary conditions which arises from linearisation, and obtain that also
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{E}_{q}^{\Omega}(T^{\ast})
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.

(7)

We may then invoke the blow-up criterion from the local-in-time existence result to find that
T^{\ast}=T_{0}
for every choice of
T_{0}>0
, thus
T_{\mathrm{max}}=\infty
, i.e. global-in-time existence.

Let us, for the moment, assume that
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{I}_{q}^{\Omega}\times\mathbb{I}_{q}^{\Sigma}
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
To show global existence, i.e.
T_{\mathrm{max}}=+\infty
, it suffices to show that there is a strong solution on each finite time interval
[0,T_{0}]
, where
T_{0}>0
can be chosen arbitrary large.
Thus, let
T_{0}>0
be arbitrary and consider
T^{\ast}=\min\{T_{0},T_{\mathrm{max}}\}
.
Then, by definition of
T_{\mathrm{max}}
, the strong solution exists on
[0,T^{\ast})
, and for each
T\in(0,T^{\ast})
, its restriction to times
t\in[0,T]
is in the class
\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Omega}(T)\times\mathbb{E}_{p}^{\Sigma}(T)
.
Moreover, from the local-in-time existence result for our class of reaction-diffusion-advection-sorption systems, employing the blow-up criterion and Corollary
9.7
, we may conclude that

T^{\ast}=T_{\mathrm{max}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\limsup_{t\rightarrow T^{\ast}}\big\|(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega)\times\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Sigma)}=\infty\quad\text{for some }q\in(1,\infty)

and, therefore, it remains to prove that
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\BUC([0,T^{\ast});\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega)^{N}\times\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Sigma)^{N})
for some
q\in(1,\infty)
.

As a preliminary step, note that
(\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\in\BUC([0,T^{\ast});\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega;\R^{N})\times\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma;\R^{N}))
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
In fact, by Lemma
9.3
and Lemma
9.8
, for every
p\in(1,\infty]
there is a constant
C_{p,T^{\ast}}>0
such that

\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq C_{p,T^{\ast}}\quad\text{for every }\tau\in(0,T^{\ast}).

By the polynomial boundedness of the chemical reaction rates and Remark
5.8
, thus

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq M^{\Omega}(1+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Omega_{\tau})})^{\gamma^{\Omega}}\leq C_{p\gamma^{\Omega},T^{\ast}},\quad

\displaystyle\tau\in(0,T^{\ast}),

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{r}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq M^{\Omega}(1+\big\|\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p\gamma^{\Omega}}(\Sigma_{\tau})})^{\gamma^{\Sigma}}\leq C_{p\gamma^{\Sigma},T^{\ast}},\quad

\displaystyle\tau\in(0,T^{\ast}).

Moreover, by the partial lower and upper linear bounds on the sorption model,

\big\|s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i}|_{\Sigma},c_{i}^{\Sigma})\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}\big\|c_{i}|_{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}+k_{i}^{\mathrm{de}}\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leq C_{p,T^{\ast}},\quad\tau\in(0,T^{\ast}).

By
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity of the linearised reaction-diffusion-system on the surface

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\Sigma}-d_{i}\Delta_{\Sigma}c_{i}^{\Sigma}=F_{i}^{\Sigma}+G_{i}^{\Sigma}&\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau},\\
c_{i}^{\Sigma}(0,\cdot)=c_{i}^{\Sigma,0}&\text{on }\Sigma\end{cases}

for
F_{i}^{\Sigma}:=r_{i}^{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma})
and
G_{i}^{\Sigma}:=s_{i}^{\Sigma}(c_{i}|_{\Sigma},c_{i}^{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})
, we may conclude that

\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{\tau})}\leq C\big(\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma,0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Sigma)}+\big\|F_{i}^{\Sigma}+G_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma_{\tau})}\big)\leq C_{q,T^{\ast}}\quad\text{for all }\tau\in(0,T^{\ast}).

Hence,
c_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T^{\ast}})
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
and
i=1,\ldots,N
, which, in particular, already implies that

\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\ast})}\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Sigma)}<\infty,

as
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T^{\ast}})\hookrightarrow\BUC([0,T^{\ast});\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega))
embeds continuously.

With this knowledge at hand, let us take a look on the reaction-diffusion-advection-system in the bulk phase

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c_{i}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-d_{i}\Delta c_{i}=F_{i}&\text{in }\Omega_{T^{\ast}},\\
k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1-\frac{c_{i}^{\Sigma,+}}{c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty}})^{+}c_{i}+d_{i}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}c_{i}=G_{i}&\text{on }\Sigma_{T^{\ast}},\\
c_{i}(0,\cdot)=c_{i}^{0}&\text{on }\overline{\Omega},\end{cases}

where
F_{i}:=r_{i}^{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{c})
and
G_{i}:=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}c_{i}^{\Sigma}
.
Since
\beta_{i}:=k_{i}^{\mathrm{ad}}(1-\frac{c_{i}^{\Sigma,+}}{c_{i}^{\Sigma,\infty}})^{+}\in\bigcap_{q>1}\mathrm{W}_{q}^{1}(0,T^{\ast};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Sigma))\cap\mathrm{L}_{q}(0,T^{\ast};\mathrm{W}_{\infty}^{1}(\Sigma))
(as we already know that
c_{i}^{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Sigma_{T^{\ast}})
for all
q\in(1,\infty)
), by
[
30
]
this problem admits
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-maximal regularity for all
q\in(1,\infty)
:
Indeed, to satisfy condition (SB) in
[
30
]
, we need to ensure that there are
s_{i0},r_{i0}\geq p
with
\frac{2}{s_{i0}}+\frac{d-1}{r_{i0}}<1-\frac{1}{p}-1=-\frac{1}{p}
such that
\beta_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{s_{i0}}^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p}}((0,T^{\ast});\mathrm{L}_{r_{i0}}(\Sigma))\cap\mathrm{L}_{s_{i0}}((0,T^{\ast});\mathrm{W}_{r_{i0}}^{1-\frac{1}{p}}(\Sigma))
, which in our case is obviously true.
Therefore,
c_{i}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T^{\ast}})
with

\big\|c_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)}(\Omega_{T^{\ast}})}\leq C\big(\big\|F_{i}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega_{T^{\ast}})}+\big\|c_{i}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega)}+\big\|c_{i}^{\Sigma}\big\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q}^{(1,2)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2q})}(\Sigma_{T^{\ast}})}\big)\leq C_{p,T^{\ast}},\quad q>1.

This, in particular, implies that

(c_{i},c_{i}^{\Sigma})\in\BUC([0,T^{\ast});\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Omega)\times\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/q}(\Sigma)).

Hence, by the blow-up criterion,
T^{\ast}=T_{0}<T_{\mathrm{max}}
for each choice of
T_{0}>0
, leading to
T_{\mathrm{max}}=\infty
.

For more general initial data note that, by Lemma
9.5
,
(\boldsymbol{c}(\varepsilon,\cdot),\boldsymbol{c}^{\Sigma}(\varepsilon,\cdot))\in\bigcap_{q\in(1,\infty)}\mathbb{I}_{q}^{\Omega}\times\mathbb{I}_{q}^{\Sigma}
.
Thus we may simply consider the problem for initial data at time
t=\varepsilon>0
.
∎
